## J P M O R G A N

## Global Data Watch

0 US recession fears moderate with better growth and inflation data

• Still sticky inflation should give more pause to European central banks

• Weak July credit and growth data prompt another China downgrade

• Up next: DM Aug flash PMIs (fcst: rotation); Riks -25bp; BI, BoK hold

## It takes a steady hand

Change is always hard. This includes periods of transition in the business cycle. Distinguishing between a slide into recession from a mid-cycle slowdown is one of the more difficult tasks in forecasting. Standing at such a juncture in this fouryear old global expansion, it is understandable to feel uneasy. In these difficult times, it is best to fall back on first principles: recessions are typically breaks in behavior driven by an adverse shock that interacts with a set of imbalanced macroeconomic conditions built up over the cycle. While the most rapid and synchronized hiking cycle in over half a century is clearly a large shock, we have long maintained that the lack of macroeconomic imbalances would short-circuit the fall into recession. Recent data are now challenging this view as a slowing US labor market, a loss of momentum in Europe, and a struggling China combine to threaten the life of the expansion. The risk is that the duration of the rate-shock has fueled its own imbalances. Credit pressures are rising, even if not to alarming levels. More concerning, however, are the continued imbalances in global growth across sectors and regions.

The global economy has outperformed this year by a large margin, again. In our year-ahead outlook published in November 2023, our soft-landing forecast called for global GDP to expand 2% this year. Global GDP is now tracking a stronger 2.4% gain for the year, with the first half expanding 0.5%-pt (ar) more than expected. Unfortunately, economic activity continues to be concentrated across sector and region. All of the outperformance this year has owed to a much stronger showing in non-manufacturing activity while manufacturing is stuck in low gear (Figure 1). At the same time, nearly all of the upside surprise has come from the US, where our rolling year-ahead outlook has been marked up over 1%-pt this year (Figure 2). Euro area GDP has also surprised somewhat to the upside, though this momentum has lost steam in recent months. And while China started the year strong, expectations have been trimmed considerably over the last quarter in response to a string of weak data, including this week’s disappointing credit and activity reports for July.

While global growth imbalances remain a concern, the focus of worry has shifted to the US of late. A notable rise in the unemployment rate alongside signs of a shift

from a slowdown in hiring to a rise in job separations has lifted recession probabilities and sent a shudder through markets. And yet, despite a moderation in job growth, the US now looks on track to post yet another quarter of above-trend 2%ar growth—above our forecast for a downshift to 1.25%. After this week’s surprisingly robust retail sales report, real consumer goods spending looks to be surging nearly 6%ar on a 3m3m, saar basis—contrasting with the recent rise in recession risks (Figure 3).

Figure 3: US recession probability and real goods spending
![](images/d6290085f33c8be2eb7ccb058ca0c22d9a0b07a4382adaa42e60dbbc78f6a41f.jpg)
Source: BEA, J.P. Morgan

Against the backdrop of resilient US growth, the anticipated aggressive shift in the Fed’s policy stance should reinforce the soft-landing outlook. This week’s US CPI report showing core inflation falling to 3.2%oya (its slowest pace since 2021) provides a green light to start the easing cycle, even if the resilience in growth tempers the chances of opening with a 50bp move (JPM fcst). The decision will likely depend on how big a bounce back the August payroll report delivers but also on the underlying signals for income generation. Something in the 160-200k range and a u-rate that ticks lower would likely erase much of the fear invoked by the July labor report, while something in the low-100s would likely tip the risk balance further toward growth and deliver a larger cut. At the same time, seeing a steadying out in the flows into unemployment will also be important to calm fears.

If our forecast is right and downside risks do not materialize, the easing in broader financial conditions of late around the world will provide fuel for global growth. The breadth of this relief however remains unclear. Next week’s flash PMIs for August will be keenly watched to see if the rotational concerns are allayed. We look for some modest improvement, with particular emphasis on a nudge up in Europe and in the DM manufacturing index.

## W. Europe: You can’t get there from here

Despite varied growth backdrops, elevated inflation across Europe is keeping central banks in the region cautious. Economic activity in the Euro area is being held back by Germany where GDP was reported to have contracted last quarter even as the rest of the region rose a more solid 1.6%ar. Continued gains in jobs, which advanced 0.9%ar in 2Q, should help boost lackluster consumer spending—particularly when combined with strong wage gains. However, the risk is that ongoing soft productivity reflects a structural malaise that damps future income and spending and keeps inflation elevated—tying the hands of the ECB from delivering as many cuts as it would like.

In the UK, GDP posted another sturdy 2.3%ar gain, continuing the rebound from its 2H23 contraction. We revise up current-quarter growth by 0.5%-pt to 1.5%ar. Solid growth alongside the highest core inflation in the DM is expected to keep the BoE to a modest quarterly pace of rate cuts with the next one coming in November. The impact of high rates has been felt the most in Sweden. With growth weakening and inflation nearing target, we expect the Riksbank to cut 25bp next week with guidance toward 3-4 cuts before year-end (2-3 previously). By contrast, Norges Bank remained on hold this week as Western Europe’s hawkish outlier. Inflation has moved steadily lower this year but remains elevated. The weak currency is preventing a more dovish pivot, and the risks to our call for a December rate cut are now tilted toward a later move.

## Stall in CE-4 disinflation limits cuts

After the rapid disinflation of 2023, July CPI releases show disinflation across Central Europe (CE-4) stalling around 4%-5%, still above central bank inflation targets (Figure 4). A reversal of earlier food price declines is also now boosting headline inflation. Czechia stands out with the lowest inflation in the region and near target. Hungary’s core inflation remains the most stubborn. Services inflation remains elevated across the region at 7%ar, reflecting high wage settlements and inertia in some industries (like telecoms and insurance).

![](images/dfdba2ae338b41742cf09afc3f20ca5e1ef0747b424f0047ed75731a0184d6e8.jpg)

Against the backdrop of still-elevated inflation, we see limited space for policy easing. With core inflation stuck above the 1.5%-3.5% target band, the NBP’s resolute hawkishness makes sense and we see it maintaining the policy rate at 5.75% until 2Q25. Likewise, we see the NBR on hold at 6.5% until February next year. Hungary’s NBH is likely to pause this month after having delivered 15 consecutive cuts although we still see it cutting 50bp later this year, to 6.25%. Despite having inflation close to target, the CNB still leans hawkish and we expect only 50bp of cuts to 3.5% by yearend.

Elsewhere in the EMEA EM region, domestic demand in Türkiye is slowing, in line with our recession call (here and here). Industrial production plummeted 3.9%q/q (not annualized) in 2Q24 and retail sales fell 0.5%. This came alongside a 0.7%-pt jump in the unemployment rate to 9.2% in June. A negative output gap should aid the disinflation process. However, the backward indexation of education and transportation prices is likely to keep services inflation elevated in the near term, with monthly CPI gains likely to hold above 2% through 3Q24. We continue to see the CBRT cutting policy rates 250bp each in November and December to 45% by year-end.

## China: Revising down 3Q growth

A weak set of activity and credit data for July prompt us to lower our 3Q growth forecast to 3%ar (previously 4%). A fall in July exports was accompanied by a stall in IP gains last month, while infrastructure investment grew at its weakest pace since 2022. Retail sales and service consumption picked up in July, but not enough to reverse the weak June reading. Renewed weakness in housing transactions and starts suggests the real estate downturn has yet to stabilize. Credit supply has been adjusted over the course of the year to provide more support, but demand remains weak, with July credit growth posting a very weak outturn. We maintain that more forceful policy measures are needed to boost consumption, stabilize the housing market, and restore private sector confidence. We still look for GDP growth to pick up toward 5% in 4Q24 as policymakers accelerate issuance of special local government bonds and provide more flexibility in the deployment of these funds. However, given the gradual policy adjustment, we expect full-year growth to average just 4.6% (previously 4.7%), below the government’s 5% target.

## EMA (ex-China) kicks off easing cycle

Domestic macro conditions (here and here) have been calling for monetary policy easing in much of EM Asia, but fears that rate cuts could trigger FX weakness have held policymakers back. The significant repricing of near-term Fed easing allays these fears somewhat. The BSP this week cut 25bp, as we expected, and with current rates well above neutral we think it has scope to cut another 25bp next quarter and at least 200bp in total by end-2025. The case for cuts is also building in India as July inflation gapped lower to 3.5%oya (on track to undershoot the RBI’s 3Q forecast) and 2Q GDP growth

likely slowed more than expected. In Indonesia, the austere 2025 budget plan presented this week, which should bring the deficit down to 2.5% of GDP (from 2.7%), is an important signal of the incoming administration’s fiscal prudence and further reinforces the case for BI to cut the policy rate by September. Political uncertainty spiked briefly in Thailand this week after the Constitutional Court dismissed Prime Minister Srettha for breaching the ethical code and the daughter of former Prime Minister Thaksin Shinawatra, Paetongtarn Shinawatra, was confirmed as the next prime minister. We do not expect any meaningful impact on fiscal or economic policies and see the BoT on hold till 2025.

## The push and pull on Latam policy

Policy-setting in Brazil and Mexico is being buffeted by a range of domestic and foreign forces. Over the past two weeks in Brazil, the BRL went from testing its weakest levels since 2022 to appreciating over 5% and edging closer to 5.45. The move happened amid a busy period, when hawkish COPOM minutes were followed by cautious remarks from different board members, reinforcing market pricing of hikes as soon as September and contrasting with our call for stable rates until mid-2025. At the same time, several economic indicators reveal surprisingly strong growth over 2Q and more persistent core inflation in July. We have upgraded our 2024 GDP forecast to 2.9% (from 2.5%) and raised our CPI forecast to 4.2%. Despite strong growth and inflation, we maintain the BCB will remain on hold even if the odds of a hike have increased. More front-loaded cuts in the US should make room for a different discussion, while keeping rates stable for longer should be enough to bring the BCB’s inflation forecasts to levels closer to the 3% target. We think the next move in the Selic rate will be lower starting in 2Q next year.

In Mexico, growth in economic activity is slowing while inflation pressures remain. Currency pressures moderated this week, but the MXN is still 10% down this year. This will cushion the economy but also jeopardize the convergence of inflation to target. Nevertheless, with inflation nearing the upper reference pace of 4% and with the Fed now looking to move more aggressively this year, Banxico has some space to act. Next week’s minutes will be important to understand and calibrate the board’s sensitivity to the incoming data, particularly given the divided 3-2 decision to cut at the last meeting. The key to understand the bias and guidance—as the statement concluded they will continue to discuss cuts in future meetings—lies in the tight policy stance as explained by the ex-ante policy rate. Even incorporating two additional cuts this year, the real policy rate will remain above 6%, well above the neutral range determined by Banxico. We expect two more 25bp cuts this year, with the next one coming in September.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our estimate for 3Q24 global real GDP growth ticked down 0.1%-pt this week to 2.0%ar (Figure 1). Our topdown global nowcaster held at 2.0%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth.

One-third of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 3Q growth in just under half of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests global capex growth will slow to a near-stall this quarter after growing 2.8%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

Our global tracker of auto sales continued its recent upward climb with a 1.3% gain in July. Global capex growth looks to have delivered a solid last quarter, but the signal from incoming surveys suggests a stall in momentum at the start of 3Q.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds were mixed this week with front-end yields rising as better-than-expected jobless claims and retail sales data in the US saw some further fading of near-term recession fears. We continue to see 50bp cuts at the Sep and Nov meetings but, while markets are pricing in a less dovish path, we remain patient as investor positioning looks elevated and as the magnitude of the first cut will depend on the August employment report. We continue to favour 5s/30s steepeners as a lower-beta bullish trade.

In the Euro area, valuations are now close to our mediumterm targets keeping us cautious on outright duration positions, and we await better opportunities to re-enter longs. We stay neutral duration outright, hold 2Yx2Y/15Yx15Y EUR swap curve steepeners as a bullish proxy, and stay neutral on France-Germany spreads as there is a decent amount political uncertainty priced in and greater clarity is needed on the path ahead. In the UK, the data flow has pointed to some support for the BoE’s stance of a gradual and steady pace of easing. We close fronts/reds steepeners as there are signs of a change in directionality on the risk of a hard landing, stay neutral on duration and add 5s/30s SONIA steepeners as a bullish proxy. In Japan, we hold tactical shorts in 1Yx1Y swaps and 2s/10s steepeners.

In EM, after some fading of the volatility that rose sharply in previous weeks over fears over a recession and a more disorderly unwind of yen-denominated carry trades, there has been some relief provided to EM assets. Overall, we stay neutral on EM local bond duration, with OWs in India, Czechia, Poland, Türkiye and Uruguay offset by an UW in Chile as well as a short duration overlay (EM Fixed Income Focus, Aug 6<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

HY bond yields collapsed to a low since August 2022 over the past week amid a Goldilocks narrative of growth without inflation. HY bond yields and spreads declined 13bp and 15bp respectively over the past week. Leveraged loan prices have now recouped two-thirds of August’s decline and the % of loans trading above par has recovered to 34%. Direct lending volume rose to a multi-year high in July amid elevated refinancing and a 10-month high for LBO activity. While leveraged loans (LL) outstanding have stabilized in 2024, the issuer count has continued to decline, which translates into growth in the average LL issuer size (Credit Strategy Weekly Update, Aug 16<sup>th</sup>).

Euro IG credit markets are extremely compressed with very little rating downgrade risk in the price. The BBB / A spread ratio has reached a new record low of just 1.24x, even lower than during the ECB CSPP / PEPP programs in 2021, or the brief pandemic distortions in early 2020. As a result, in our view, lower-rated investment grade paper is more vulnerable to a growth scare. In spread terms, BBs have underperformed both BBBs and single-Bs over recent weeks (European Credit Weekly, Aug 16<sup>th</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/9f16a6729da435f3642582b67bd1ed259ca81f65c38631d353001bbff20fb691.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

Rising CES prepayment speeds. Along with declines in the 2yr and 5yr rates, mortgage rates could be at, or marginally below, 6% by the end of the year. If realized, this will improve affordability and potentially thaw a frozen housing market. However, we do not think this becomes a massive driver of higher home prices as supply and demand potentially rise commensurately. Our home price forecast for the remainder of the year is benign, only expecting another 1.5% of growth over the next 6 months. Closed-end second (CES) lien prepayment speeds have been picking up likely due to borrowers either paying off the loan in cash or refinancing just the second lien. This makes sense, as CES rates have come down since last year. Rates are lower due to significantly better private label execution. Loan bids continue to stay competitive as pools trade at steep premiums. However, given recent higher prepayment prints, market expectations of CES speeds will have to recalibrate (RMBS Credit Commentary,

Aug 16<sup>th</sup>).

## Currencies

Fed easing and dollar weakness, uneasy bedfellows. The onset of a Fed easing cycle is not reflexively USD bearish; USD has appreciated after the first cut in 3 of the past 4 easing cycles. Valuation and positioning differences this time around skew the baseline to the downside, but this should be considered in the context of the US’s stronger cyclical position, the mature discounting of Fed cuts, and risks to the global backdrop. Further NZD weakness will be harder to come by after RBNZ’s first cut; trim shorts.

Trade Recommendations: Take profit on short NZD/NOK after RBNZ & rotate into long NOK/SEK ahead of Riksbank. Stay short EUR vs USD & NOK in options ahead of PMIs; sell EUR/GBP in cash. Hold select USD longs (vs NZD & EUR); Jackson Hole & PMIs key event risk. EUR/CHF short stopped out at profit. We remain MW overall in EM FX as weak growth backdrop and looming US election risk contain positive impact of the Fed’s approaching rate cuts (FX Markets Weekly, Aug 16<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

China steel production is now tracking at its lowest August run-rate for the last 6 years, reinforcing the seasonal trend that China steel output for 2024 likely peaked \~2 months ago. Iron Ore prices continue to face ongoing downside risks due to weak real China steel demand, weak China steel mill profitability, very high summer temperatures that impact construction activity. However, this was partially mitigated in July, since global iron ore shipments also fell 8% vs June and were 5% lower vs Jul’23, but this specifically reflected Australian port maintenance rather than a sustained signal of supply discipline. China physical demand signals again appear more constructive in copper (Metal Inventories in China, Aug 11<sup>th</sup>).

Technicals indicate crude nearing resistance, gold setting up for a breakout, copper rebounding. Brent crude futures rebound from the key medium-term support for the second time this summer and approach resistance within a broad trading range. Gold pressures the upper end of the trading range that we view as a bullish consolidation pattern. The price action leaves us looking for an eventual release to higher prices, potentially as early as this Sep-Oct. Copper futures try to form a short-term bottom after fully retracing back to the Mar pattern breakout area. Copper looks set to mean revert to higher prices over the near-term (Commodity Technical Update, Aug 13<sup>th</sup>).

## RBA: Learning the hard way

• The RBA upgraded its trimmed mean inflation projections in the latest forecast round

• Upgrades reflect a reassessment of spare capacity

Historically, the RBA’s inflation forecasts appear overly sensitive to changes in economic activity

This potentially underestimates the inflation expectations channel

In the RBA’s August Statement of Monetary Policy (SoMP), the Bank revised up its trimmed mean inflation projections across the forecast horizon (Figure 1). The upgrades reflect both firmer domestic demand and "a judgement that the economy's capacity to meet that demand is somewhat weaker than previously thought.” The reassessment of capacity pressure mainly rests on the slower-than-expected disinflation alongside a still-tight labor market observed over the past year.

Figure 1: RBA trimmed mean inflation forecasts
![](images/b7cc1392441919f1825681980746d8818cab2b97ec45bdc4c00056b712d130e1.jpg)

Earlier in the week, Deputy Governor Hauser argued forecast errors provide useful information about the state of the world. Relative to the forecasts in the February 2023 SoMP (the peak for headline inflation), inflation has not decayed as rapidly as expected, but the unemployment rate has evolved broadly as expected (Figure 2). These outcomes suggest the labor market is further from equilibrium than initially thought, so generating excess inflation pressure. Of course, it’s also possible that slower-than-anticipated disinflation reflects a weaker relationship between unemployment and inflation than assumed in the Bank’s forecasts.

In this piece, we examine the relationships between the RBA’s forecasts of macroeconomic variables over a longer horizon. We find that labor market developments typically have less impact on inflation than assumed in the RBA’s forecasts. But higher interest rates are associated with greater declines in inflation, and smaller increases in unemployment, than the Bank’s forecasts imply.

Figure 2: RBA inflation and unemployment forecast errors %-pt difference between actual and RBA forecast
![](images/c6e7d85d973e343f1f2e9356f5871ea415dc163711440cfcb66f4688c2331021.jpg)

## Know thy weaknesses

One way to assess the relationship between inflation and economic activity embedded in the RBA’s forecast is to regress the Bank’s inflation forecast errors against its forecasts of other macroeconomic variables across different forecast horizons.<sup>1</sup>If the RBA’s forecast of a certain macroeconomic variable is systematically related to higher than forecast inflation, then the Bank’s forecasts underestimate the impact of the variable on inflation. This approach was first used by Blanchard and Leigh (2013) to assess the impact of fiscal consolidation on forecasts of growth, and more recently by the Bank of England to appraise its own forecasts.

Figure 3: Effect of unemployment forecast on inflation forecast errors %-pt inflation forecast error based on 1% increase in unemployment forecast
![](images/8697ef46997adfe4f9c31681933714b06a5d07d3037790fec396e66c30f2abe1.jpg)
Source: RBA, J.P. Morgan

Figure 3 shows the effect of the RBA’s unemployment rate forecasts on its headline CPI forecast errors at different forecast horizons (the grey regions represent the 90% confidence bands). Results are based on data from 1Q96-1Q23, and we use robust regression to account for outliers. The outcomes in Figure 3 imply that forecasts of greater unemployment are

followed by higher than anticipated inflation at the 3Q horizon and beyond. The findings are symmetric, so forecasts of lower unemployment are followed by lower than expected headline inflation over the same horizon.

Figure 4 shows that these results also hold for estimates of underlying inflation: expectations of higher (lower) unemployment are accompanied by higher (lower) than anticipated underlying inflation. These findings suggest that the RBA’s forecasts tend to overestimate the effects of unemployment on inflation, perhaps because the true slope of the Phillips curve is flatter than the forecasts assume. As we noted last month, the NAIRU was also likely lower than the Bank assumed in the second half of last decade, which would generate the observed association between expectations of lower unemployment and subsequent downside surprises on inflation.

Figure 4: Core forecast errors and unemployment forecast %-pt core inflation forecast error based on 1% increase in unemployment forecast
![](images/5ffbf500bf09477ca951728cc288d1a714246913a2af421e25aa4a5130fa4632.jpg)
Source: RBA, J.P. Morgan

Recent forecast errors appear very much in keeping with these results. However, the Bank has been more explicit in communicating the implications of the current revisions for the outlook than in the past. This might simply reflect that last year’s external review called for the RBA to display greater public introspection. But the Bank might also have more confidence that inflation dynamics reflect demand/supply imbalances than movements in inflation expectations this time around. Last cycle, falling neutral rate estimates and the ZLB constraint represented a mechanical barrier to achieving the inflation target, and so may have contributed to slippage in inflation expectations which were hard to disentangle from movements in capacity pressures.

## Rates and expectations

In the last couple of forecast rounds, more attention has been placed on the interaction between the inflation projections and the assumed interest rate path. The May SoMP noted that a higher path for the cash rate (the technical assumption used market pricing) supported the return of inflation to target. One way we can assess the monetary policy transmission mechanism implicit in the Bank’s forecasts is to run the same the regressions as above using the market’s forecast of the one year interest rate swap rate six months ahead (i.e. the 6m fwd, 1y rate) as the explanatory variable.

Figure 5: CPI forecast errors and 1y yield forecast
![](images/fd5426e210c82a035d31fd995f94a6f8376d542bcc27bead71757d612a8175ab.jpg)
Source: RBA, J.P. Morgan

Figure 5 shows that expectations of higher interest rates are associated with lower than anticipated inflation at longer horizons, while Figure 6 shows that higher interest rates are typically accompanied by lower than forecast unemployment. Taken together, these findings provide some evidence that monetary policy acts more forcefully through an expectations channel than implicit in the RBA’s forecast: higher interest rates help to anchor short-run inflation expectations. This helps lower inflation, with less direct effect on unemployment.

It is difficult to separate out the expectations channel. If the central bank sets policy to influence market expectations, and market participants themselves attempt to anticipate the central bank’s actions, this will result in a feedback loop that renders market signals uninformative. For the RBA’s part, guidance has been consistently open-ended of late, and so rates expectations have mainly fluctuated with movements in the economic outlook, while longer-run inflation expectations have remained anchored at target. We’ve argued previously that movements in short-run expectations have been an important conduit of supply shocks this cycle, and so stability of expectations might have a larger disinflationary impact than implicit in the RBA’s forecasts.

Figure 6: Unemployment forecast errors and 1y yield forecast %-pt inflation revision
![](images/7ba503cd934b6b9f4281c7d4d681484db980f80b92ef9d52032265c8b298926d.jpg)
Source: RBA, J.P. Morgan

## United States

The July CPI report was tame, as the core measure increased only 0.17%

• Firm retail sales trend led us to revise up 3Q GDP growth to 1.25%

Initial jobless claims move lower as automaker-related distortions fade

• Powell’s Jackson Hole remarks next week unlikely to tip hand for September FOMC

The immaculate disinflation thesis received more support this week, as inflation continues to moderate against a backdrop of healthy growth in economic activity. The CPI report for July was benign as the ex-food and energy core measure increased only 0.17%, and over the last year is up 3.2%. After a few unwelcome upside surprises at the beginning of the year, core inflation has been well behaved more recently, increasing at only a 1.6% annual rate over the last three months. The lagging rental measures are still slow to moderate, but excluding shelter costs core inflation was up 1.7% in the 12 months through July.

Meanwhile, most indicators of growth were generally favorable this week. Most notably, retail sales rose 1.0% in July and the core “control” measure increased 0.3%. These figures suggest real consumer spending is off to a good start in 3Q and accordingly we revised up our estimate of current-quarter GDP growth to 1.25%. We also separately rejiggered the contour of real GDP growth for 2025, effectively swapping the first-half and second-half growth profiles to reflect more modest growth in 1H25 followed by a moderately stronger rebound in 2H25 as the impact of the front-loaded Fed easing cycle starting next month percolates through the economy. This revision leaves 4Q/4Q and year-on-year growth for 2025 unchanged.

The latest jobless claims data also peeled back some of the recent increase, suggesting the deterioration in labor market conditions seen in the July employment report is not building on itself. Some of the prior rise in claims may owe to extended automaker shutdowns, a factor that also weighed on the July industrial production report released this week. State-level data suggest some drag on payrolls in Texas that might be attributed in part to Hurricane Beryl; impacted sectors showed an earlier rise in jobless claims as well, so we could see a modest bounce-back in August nonfarm payrolls. Beryl does not appear to have distorted the national unemployment rate, however, although the automaker shutdowns might have led to temporary layoffs in the Midwest.

The data flow next week slows to a trickle. Instead, the most notable development will likely be Fed chair Powell’s remarks at the annual Jackson Hole symposium. There has been only limited Fed-speak since the disappointing July jobs report. Of those who have spoken most have asserted that risks to the employment and inflation mandates are close to balanced. They generally have also judged that policy is currently restrictive. In this setting we think it makes sense to get policy back to neutral quickly and believe a 50bp reduction in the funds rate at the September FOMC meeting is warranted. That said, we doubt Chair Powell will pre-judge the outcome of that meeting, given almost a full month of data releases between now and then.

## Inflation genie gets back in the bottle

This week saw the release of the full suite of July inflation data—CPI, PPI, and import prices—and the net result is that we expect only a modest 0.12% increase in the core PCE price gauge last month. Both headline and core CPI rose 0.2% on the month, with core very close to our expectations (a realized 0.165% unrounded print versus our forecast of 0.170%) but headline slightly softer. As a result, the year-ago pace of headline CPI inflation eased a tenth to 2.9%, while core slid a tenth to 3.2% as expected (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Core CPI run rates
![](images/93ef4e9d11741d6088a843b06346a3aff617e1410e8d5310b9b8f378118bedd2.jpg)

Both of these readings mark the slowest annual pace of inflation since 2021. The unexpected softness in headline can largely be traced to a flat contribution from energy on the month, while food prices rose 0.2% as expected. Away from food and energy, the details on the core were more varied but broadly aligned with our expectations. Core services firmed 0.3% while core goods declined 0.3% for July. The former was the strongest in three months while the latter was the softest reading for core goods prices in a year.

As we forecasted, shelter prices firmed on the month—albeit a bit more than anticipated, with owners’ equivalent rent (OER) up 0.4% and tenants’ rent up 0.5% on the month (Figure 2).

Figure 2: CPI rent measures
![](images/7c69ba84bd9689091857e63f034a2c847b77e4f16efab9edd8dc1c39449eb514.jpg)

New and used vehicle prices eased broadly as forecast as well, with the latter accelerating their recent monthly pace of decline to 2.3%. Industry data point to a reversal of this drag in coming months. Airfares (down 1.6%) and postage (up 0.8%) also provided offsetting but broadly anticipated swings last month. Motor vehicle insurance costs appear to have resumed their upward trend after a brief interruption in May; they rose 1.2% in July. More surprising was the 0.3% drop in medical care services, which appears to have been nearly entirely due to an unexpected 1.9% decline in outpatient hospital services. The rest of the subcomponents for medical services rose last month, and several accelerated.

The July producer price index (PPI) report was generally softer than expected, re-establishing a disinflationary trend for producers’ prices after some firming earlier this year. The headline final demand measure rose 0.1% on the month, while the core PPI index (excluding food and energy) was unchanged. With revisions to prior months, year-ago headline PPI inflation has cooled to 2.2% from an upwardly-revised 2.7% in June, while core eased to 2.4%oya from 3.0% in June. Although the pass-through from producer into consumer prices is incomplete and of variable length, the July reading is well within the range that allows the Fed to continue to place its primary focus on the labor market at upcoming policy decisions.

## Consumer spending stays firm

The July retail sales report came in strong and points to another solid gain in real consumer spending in 3Q. Based on the retail data as well as a reported decline in utilities in the IP report, our expectation is that real PCE rose 0.2%-0.3%m/m in July. Even if there were no further gains in spending in August and September, real PCE for the quarter would still increase a solid 2.1%q/q, saar.

Total retail sales increased 1.0%m/m in July, supported by a 3.6%m/m increase in sales at motor vehicle and parts dealers. These data are consistent with a 4.2%m/m rebound in unit vehicle sales reported for July. Excluding autos and gas, total sales rose 0.4%. The control category, which further excludes food services and building materials, rose a high-side 0.3%, which comes after a large 0.9% increase the prior month. The control category rose 4.9%3m/3m, saar, reversing a slowdown earlier in the year (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Retail sales and food services %3m/3m, saar
![](images/73553f6024ae59587c81cfefb2172ae46627e9aafccdfd85dae702b3f875551e.jpg)

We had expected food services to be a drag on sales given the signal from Chase card data, but in fact that category rose 0.3%m/m. That said, the 2% annualized increase over the last three months is still a slow pace. Results for housing-related components were also decent, as sales at building material stores rose 0.9% while furniture, electronic, and appliance store sales increased 1.0% in July. Sales at both of these store types have trended upward from earlier in the year after being weak last year. Sales in some of the other larger categories, including grocery stores, general merchandise stores, and e-commerce, were all up on the month as well.

Stronger than expected retail sales data for July paint a relatively upbeat picture for consumers, but the preliminary August print of the University of Michigan’s consumer sentiment index showed only a mild improvement that was driven by the expectations component while current conditions index slid a bit. Inflation expectations remained unchanged both at the one-year (2.9%) and five-year (3.0%) horizons.

## Housing starts slide

Housing starts and permits declined substantially in July, with starts down 7%m/m to 1.238mn saar, and permits down 4% to 1.396mn saar. Moreover, there were net downward revisions to starts in prior months. The decline in total starts was driven by a 14% plunge in the single-family segment, which left them near their cycle lows from early 2022. Despite modest declines in mortgage interest rates that are likely to continue as the Fed easing cycle commences, we think residential investment growth in the GDP accounts should be soft in 2H given the past declines and given that units under construction are still falling from elevated levels. In addition, builders have been giving a lot of rate incentives to buyers, which they may withdraw as mortgage rates decline.

Payroll survey week</td></tr></table>

We project initial jobless claims in the week ending August 17 to be about unchanged at 230k vs. 225k the prior week. Jobless claims have fallen by 23k over the last two weeks, in part stemming from claims returning to more normal levels in Texas following Hurricane Beryl and Michigan following the re-opening of auto plants that had been on summer shutdown. As we wrote last week there could be some residual seasonality that contributed to the increase in claims in May and June, with claims then further propped up by Texas and Michigan last month. If we replicate the experience of last year than claims could fall under 220k by early September. Currently seasonally adjusted claims are already running about 8% below last year’s level though, which is one reason we expect them to not go lower just yet.

Note that the week ending August 17<sup>th</sup> will be the payroll survey, which is the week including the 12<sup>th</sup> of the month. If our forecast is realized claims would be down 15k from the July survey week, though the four-week average of claims would be unchanged at 235k.

Continuing claims will also be key to watch. They increased substantially between late May and late June, and since then their rate of increase has slowed but it is not clear if claims are leveling off.

<table><tr><td rowspan="3">Wed Jul 24 9:45am</td><td>S&amp;P Global manufacturing PMI (US flash)</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Index, sa</td><td>Aug 23</td><td>Jul 24 Aug 24</td></tr><tr><td>Composite</td><td>47.9</td><td>49.6</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Output</td><td>48.5</td><td>49.0 50.5</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>ISM-weighted composite</td><td>47.5</td><td>49.7</td></tr></table>

We expect the manufacturing PMI to move modestly lower to 49.0 in the August flash report from 49.6 in July. The gap

between the PMI and ISM manufacturing surveys has recently widened, with the ISM returning near its cycle low whereas the PMI survey has generally improved, despite a decline in July. As for the two regional Fed manufacturing surveys out this month, both the Empire and Philadelphia Fed measures recorded declines in their weighted composites. The average of the two is still stronger than a few months ago stemming from strength in the Philadelphia Fed composite, though we are discounting that a bit in light of that survey having been considerably stronger than other manufacturing surveys recently.

<table><tr><td rowspan="3">Wed Jul 24 9:45am</td><td colspan="4">S&amp;P Global services PMI (US flash)</td></tr><tr><td>Index, sa</td><td>Aug 23</td><td>Jul 24</td><td>Aug 24</td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>50.5</td><td>55.0</td><td>54.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Employment</td><td>50.4</td><td>51.7</td><td></td></tr></table>

We look for the general business activity index of the services PMI to decline to 54.0 in the August flash survey from 55.0 in July. The business activity measure in this survey has often run below the ISM survey, but the two measures are now at very similar levels, as the ISM survey has generally trended down whereas the PMI survey has improved. That has left the ISM survey looking weak relative to its own history, whereas the PMI is in better shape. As for other August service sector surveys, the Business Leaders Survey from the NY Fed rose in August and has mirrored the improvement over the last couple of years in the PMI survey.

When the final July PMI came out there was a 1-pt decline between the flash and final surveys, which we speculated could signal a weakening in the latter half of the month that would become more apparent in August. However, this is not the basis for our August projection, as upon further investigation there is no evident correlation between the change in the flash and final survey in one month, and how the flash survey changes in the following month.

<table><tr><td>Thu Aug 22 10:00am</td><td>Existing home sales</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>Total (mn, saar)</td><td>4.14</td><td>4.11</td><td>3.89</td><td>3.95</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>-1.9</td><td>-0.7</td><td>-5.4</td><td>2.0</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>6.8</td><td>-0.7</td><td>-13.4</td><td>-2.5</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply (nsa)</td><td>3.5</td><td>3.7</td><td>4.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Single-family</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.6</td><td>4.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>5.4</td><td>5.2</td><td>4.1</td><td></td></tr></table>

Existing home sales should rise around 2.0%m/m in July to 3.95mn annualized pace, which would still be very low. Pending home sales, which lead existing home sales by 1-2 months, rose 5%m/m in June after having hit an all-time low the prior month. Existing home sales could strengthen a bit more in coming months in response to lower interest rates, though this has yet to become evident in other high-frequency data. While mortgage refinance applications did recently jump in the MBA’s weekly numbers, purchase applications were lower in July than in June and have only increased marginally in the last couple weeks.

<table><tr><td>Fri New home sales Aug 23 10:00am Apr May Jun Jul Total (000s,saar) 730 621 617 610</td></tr></table>

We predict that new single-family home sales declined 1%m/ m to a 610k annualized pace in July. Other indicators related to the new home market were soft in July, with single-family housing starts declining 14% and the NAHB housing market index falling (and then falling again in August). While new home sales have held in much better than existing home sales, spurred in part by builder incentives such as mortgage rate buydowns, they have been edging lower over most of the last year with the exception of a spike in April.

In addition to the sales figures, the inventory data will be key to watch, since higher inventory has likely spurred builders to cut starts recently. The absolute number of homes for sale is the highest since early 2008, and the months’ supply of new homes is likewise elevated. On the positive side though, the share of inventory which has already been completed is only now returning to pre-COVID levels, and the median number of months that a completed home has been on the market is around all-time lows.

The July producer price index (PPI) report was generally softer than expected, re-establishing a disinflationary trend for producers’ prices after some firming earlier this year. The headline final demand measure rose 0.1% on the month, while the core PPI index (excluding food and energy) was unchanged. With revisions to prior months, year-ago headline

PPI inflation has cooled to 2.2% from an upwardly-revised 2.7% in June, while core eased to 2.4%oya from 3.0% in June. Although the pass-through from producer into consumer prices is incomplete and of variable length, this report was well within the range that allows the Fed to continue to place its primary focus on the labor market at upcoming policy decisions.

The July CPI report was broadly in line with expectations for a further cooling in inflation that keeps the Fed on track to begin easing at its upcoming September meeting. With Chair Powell having put the FOMC’s focus firmly on the labor market, the August jobs report will be the key determinant of how much the Fed cuts next month. We look for a 50bp start to the easing cycle given that the current level of restrictive policy looks increasingly off-sides against cooling data and more balanced risks.

Both headline and core CPI rose 0.2% on the month, with core very close to our expectations (a realized 0.165% unrounded print versus our forecast of 0.170%) but headline slightly softer. As a result, the year-ago pace of headline CPI inflation eased a tenth to 2.9%, while core slid a tenth to 3.2% as expected. Both of these readings mark the slowest annual pace of inflation since 2021. The unexpected softness in headline can largely be traced to a flat contribution from energy on the month, while food prices rose 0.2% as expected. Away from food and energy, the details on the core were more varied but broadly aligned with our expectations. Core services firmed 0.3% while core goods declined 0.3% for July. The former was the strongest in three months while the latter was the softest reading for core goods prices in a year.

Import prices were a touch firmer than expected in July. The headline import price index, as well as an index that excludes imported fuels prices, increased 0.1% last month. As a result, the year-ago inflation rates for both series ticked up a tenth, to 1.7% on the headline and 1.2% on the ex-fuels measure. We have been looking for lagged appreciation of the tradeweighted US dollar to start to weigh on import prices, but that is not yet apparent in the data. Some of that effect may be attenuated going forward as the trade-weighted dollar has moved off its recent highs since the end of July following a repricing of the path for Fed easing.

Conditions</td><td>1.3</td><td>13.9</td><td>8.0 -7.0</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>-2.2</td><td>20.7</td><td>14.6</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>-7.2</td><td>27.8</td><td>8.5</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>-6.4</td><td>-9.4</td><td>-4.8</td></tr><tr><td>Prices paid</td><td>22.5</td><td>19.8</td><td>24.0</td></tr><tr><td>Prices received</td><td>13.7</td><td>24.2</td><td>13.7</td></tr><tr><td>Composite</td><td>47.2</td><td>56.3</td><td>52.7</td></tr></table>

The manufacturing surveys for August were on balance a bit weaker than the prior month but don’t signal any sharp changes in trend. The general business activity indexes in the Empire State and Philadelphia Fed surveys were mixed, though the ISM-weighted composites in both surveys declined. The Empire State activity index rose from -6.6 to -4.7, while the ISM-weighted composite dipped from 48.0 to 47.2 in response to weaker readings on new orders, shipments, and inventories. For the Philadelphia Fed survey, the business activity index plunged from 13.9 to -7.0, the lowest since January, but the decline in the weighted composite was not as bad, as it fell from an elevated 56.3 to 52.7, which absent last month is still the best reading in two years. The Philadelphia Fed survey had been stronger than other surveys over recent months, especially in July, so it is not surprising to see it weaken.

Industrial production (Aug 15) %m/m,sa,unless noted

A big drop in the volatile auto production category held down IP in July, even as manufacturing output ex autos continues to rise. Total IP registered a sizable 0.6%m/m decline, with manufacturing falling 0.3%, mining flat, and utilities down 3.7%. The manufacturing fall was led by a 7.8% decrease in motor vehicle and parts, and excluding that manufacturing was up 0.3%. Manufacturing ex autos, which has increased 5% annualized in the last three months, has generally been trending up since the start of the year after having declined over the prior couple years. The drop in auto production was likely related to unusually large summer auto plant shutdowns, which appeared to have also contributed to a rise in jobless claims in Michigan and an increase in temporary layoffs recorded in the July employment report.

Homebuilders survey (Aug 15)
<table><tr><td>Sa</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td>Aug</td><td rowspan="3">39</td></tr><tr><td>Housing market</td><td>43</td><td>42</td><td>42</td></tr><tr><td>Present sales</td><td>48</td><td>47</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Prospective buyer traffic</td><td>28</td><td>27</td><td></td></tr></table>

The homebuilder index weakened again in August, with the NAHB housing market index falling to 39 from 41, which is better than the cycle low of 31 but down from a recent peak of 51 in April. Recent declines in mortgage rates could stimulate sales a bit in coming months and the index of expected sales in the next six months edged up slightly to 49 from 48, but it is probably too soon to see much of an effect on actual sales, and indeed the indexes for present sales and prospective buyer traffic, which make up most of the weight in the index, both declined.

Housing starts and permits declined substantially in July, with starts down 7%m/m and permits down 4%, and there were some net downward revisions to starts in prior months. The starts decline wasn’t just driven by the volatile multifamily segment, which was actually up 14%; rather single-family starts plunged 14%, which takes them back down near their cycle lows from early 2022. Single-family home inventories have been rising and high-frequency signals (weekly mortgage purchase applications, the August NAHB survey) haven’t yet pointed to any substantive improvement in sales, which is likely driving builders to pull back. This doesn’t necessarily mean that single-family starts need to keep falling now that they are down 25% from their recent Feb 2024 high against a backdrop of recent declines in mortgage rates. But residential investment growth in the GDP accounts should be soft in 2H given the past declines and given that units under construction are still falling from elevated levels.

Consumer sentiment (Aug 16)
<table><tr><td colspan="2">Michigan preliminary</td><td rowspan="2">Jul</td><td rowspan="2">Aug</td><td rowspan="2"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Univ. of Mich. Index (nsa)</td><td>68.2</td><td>66.4</td><td>65.0</td><td>67.8</td></tr><tr><td>Current conditions</td><td>65.9</td><td>62.7</td><td></td><td>60.9</td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>69.6</td><td>68.8</td><td></td><td>72.1</td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td>3.0</td><td>2.9</td><td></td><td>2.9</td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td></td><td>3.0</td></tr></table>

The Michigan consumer sentiment index edged up to 67.8 in the preliminary August report from 66.4 in July. The improvement was entirely concentrated in expectations, as the current conditions index fell to 60.9 from 62.7. The press release noted the significant role that election news played this month. The survey period ran from July 23 to August 12, meaning it began just after President Biden withdrew from the race. This caused a rebound in sentiment among Democratic respondents, though only back to the June level, whereas expectations for Republicans was hardly changed. In contrast, both groups reported weaker current conditions, with the market volatility following the release of the July payroll report possibly playing a role.

## Focus: benchmark to revise down payrolls modestly

The BLS will release the preliminary annual benchmark revision for payroll (CES) employment on August 21. We expect a downward revision of 360K to the level of employment as of March 2024, or roughly 30K per month between March 2023 and March 2024. If realized, that would still leave solid payroll gains of about 220K per month over that period. The final benchmark revision for payrolls through March 2024 will be published next February. In addition to finalizing the changes discussed above, that revision also will update the birth-death factors for April to December 2024 (yielding new NSA growth rates) and refresh the seasonal factors for the last five years. Looking ahead to future revisions for April 2024 to March 2025, we see a risk for payroll gains to be revised down slightly in the following year’s benchmark revision.

Figure 1: Nonfarm employment benchmark revisions Revision to CES March employment %oya growth for specified benchmark year
![](images/7ff440153bfc6097130388612d5ab8e70967f56ac908a843f89cba957e1e5617.jpg)
Source: BLS, J.P. Morgan

The main data used for the benchmark is the Quarterly Census of Employment and Wages (QCEW), currently available through December. In the 12 months to December 2023, QCEW employment grew 0.45%-pt less than the CES. We previously used this miss to forecast that March 2024 employment would be revised down, by comparing the size of past revisions to the size of the gap between QCEW and CES growth rates as of the prior December.

Previously we used data since 2007, which we now extend back to the late 1970s (Figure 1). The longer series allows us to see whether the state of the labor market also influences the magnitude of the revision. To measure this, we regress revisions against both the QCEW-CES gap in December and the March-to-March change in the unemployment rate. We find that a rise in the unemployment rate change is associated with a more negative payroll revision, holding the QCEW-CES gap constant. To help visualize this, Figure 1 shows the forecast of the regression for different values of the QCEW-CES gap but with the 12-month unemployment rate increase held constant at its March 2024 value of +0.8%-pt. A larger unemployment rate increase would shift the line down. Using this new model with the longer dataset yields the forecast for a -360k revision, smaller than our original estimate of -500k. The 68% prediction interval is roughly -720k to 0k.

A longer time series also shows whether annual revisions are correlated across time. If unemployment rate changes influence revisions we would expect this, since unemployment rate changes are often serially correlated. We indeed find that consecutive revisions in the same direction were frequent through the Great Recession, although they have been less common since then (Figure 2). Note that a new CES sampling procedure and birth/death model was introduced in the early 2000s, which could change the pattern. Consistent with the point that unemployment increases are associated with more negative revisions, we find that almost all years with rising unemployment rates have negative revisions.

Figure 2: Annual payroll revisions, March of prior year to current year %oya growth
![](images/910a4e1d53020a2bee9ac0080d08ced5cec73f30c0fc0a49f900d29c56b1eb82.jpg)

Turning to what might influence revisions after March 2024, the CES forecasted birth/death adjustment in the past 12 months has been adding about 15k per month more jobs than pre-pandemic, which as discussed in prior research could be an overstatement. Business Employment Dynamics data through 4Q23 show net employment growth from opening less closing establishments has already returned to pre-COVID levels, suggesting that the birth/death adjustment in the CES framework should follow a similar pattern (Figure 3). The gap between the current forecasted CES birth/adjustment adjustment and pre-COVID average has been shrinking though, so this problem may become less material over time.

Figure 3: Net job creation from establishment birth/death
![](images/35b53758cd438ad7db2f5776ece0b886ff59cd505f3278f17c5e3079b049cffe.jpg)

## Euro Area

• Euro area GDP gain revised up modestly in 2Q24 to 1.2%q/q saar

• This makes ex-Germany GDP look more solid

Employment also gained solidly in 2Q24, with productivity a tick better

• Euro area IP remains weak however

This week’s official activity data have been modestly encouraging. Euro area GDP growth was revised up in 2Q24 by 0.2%-pt to 1.2%q/q saar. This is a small change, but leaves GDP ex-Germany and ex-Ireland up by a stronger 1.6%q/q saar. In addition, the flash estimate of Euro area employment showed a solid gain of 0.9%q/q saar in 2Q24, which implies a return to modestly positive labor productivity growth. And, while the IP report for June was still weak, it implies that the broader economy continues to drive the gains in real GDP. This is also consistent with the PMI, which continues to show a big gap between manufacturing and services.

Despite all of this, our focus is firmly on next week’s flash PMI for August. After back-to-back declines, its growth signal has slipped back to below a 1%ar, mainly due to a relapse in manufacturing. Our GDP forecast requires the PMI to stabilize and even nudge a tick higher. We are inclined to think that this is possible already in August, but risks are likely on the downside in light of the recent shift in US growth risks and market repricing. The German ZEW, which we tend to not put too much weight on as it is a survey of analysts in financial markets, has signalled a decline in the IFO. Either way, the PMI will be a crucial report in the run-up to the ECB’s next policy meeting in September. We continue to think that the ECB will continue with a quarterly pace of cuts, which implies a 25bp cut in September and a skip in October. But, we have also signalled that October could come into play if the PMI falls significantly further and the inflation data show a clearer slowing in services inflation.

## Slightly improved GDP and jobs news

This week’s second estimate of 2Q24 GDP came with a first estimate of employment. Both were modestly encouraging, but without removing the concern about the weakening of the PMI into 3Q24.

Euro area GDP growth was revised up modestly from 1.0%q/ q saar to 1.2%q/q saar (Figure 1). The revision was largely due to the Netherlands, which grew a strong 4%q/q saar in 2Q24. This means that excluding Germany (weak) and Ireland (noisy), the region grew at a solid 1.6%ar in 2Q24, up from 1.3%ar at the time of the flash report. In addition, national data point to some modest upward revisions to prior quarters, but Eurostat’s convention is to not incorporate these until the third estimate of GDP, when all details are published. Hence, at this stage, we still only have a single number for 2Q, i.e. headline real GDP. We also do not yet have details to assess the German weakness in 2Q. At a contraction of 0.3%q/q saar, Germany is clearly an outlier to the downside, whereas the rest of the region grew at an above-potential pace. While that is encouraging, the focus is firmly on next week’s flash PMI to give a steer on the region’s performance after back-to-back declines in June and July.

Figure 1: Tracking Euro area GDP growth with the PMI %q/q saar, model with dummies for 4Q08, 1Q09 and 3Q10-4Q12
![](images/f63ad74e910bdf4ab178f5e6abc7a89752f6f49d61326331a0122b1ffaaaa69a.jpg)

The second estimate of 2Q24 GDP came alongside a first estimate of employment. This showed an increase of 0.9%q/q saar in 2Q24, a tick firmer than we had thought based on national data, but closer to our original forecast. The PMI employment index suggests a modest slowing at the start of 3Q24, but this is not yet dramatic (Figure 2). The same applies to unemployment data, which have barely ticked up. Hence, labour market conditions are still pretty solid so far.

Figure 2: Euro area employment

%q/q saar, model estimated over pre-2008 period

![](images/8a8a1dd09a53a8fe2ed9d4dc1dc1f293d438275a43d3cd7243b1705f1688979c.jpg)

The implication of the 1.2%q/q saar gain in real GDP and the 0.9%q/q saar gain in employment is that output per worker advanced 0.3%q/q saar in 2Q24. This is the first increase in six quarters, but leaves the level still depressed (Figure 3). Output per hour worked has performed better, but an estimate of this is not yet available for 2Q24. We note also that the PMI suggests no real underlying improvement, including at the start of 3Q24 (Figure 4).

Figure 3: Euro area labour productivity
![](images/f692ff258dcd8daf8c685c14602439b9d1d79c90a33a09117c08ffaafe35c100.jpg)

Figure 4: Euro area labour productivity
![](images/2dfefda39daf6e1ce12f89867a9e7df041ffd9da2e8ff14a6afd228678d9b771.jpg)

In terms of the supply side, the last two quarters have posted a delivered potential of around 1%q/q saar, which is a significant improvement (Figure 5). However, little of this is has been driven by productivity. Instead, a lot of it has come from the growth of the labor force, which has been surprisingly solid (Figure 6). Our labor force proxy did slow a bit in 2Q24, but it continues to be firmer than suggested by the underlying demographics and is therefore still likely being boosted by immigration. The relative weakness in output per person means that the modest GDP growth in the region has been more job-intensive than is normally the case. This is a key reason why the unemployment rate remains so low despite the subdued growth performance.

## Euro area IP recovery only modest in June

Euro area IP declined 0.1%m/m in June, with manufacturing down 0.3%m/m. On a more meaningful ex-Ireland basis, IP was up 0.6%m/m and by around 0.5%m/m in manufacturing (Figure 7). On an ex-Ireland basis, this left 2Q24 with a declines of around 3%q/q saar. While we do not know the GDP details, this does suggest that services continued to expand in 2Q24, driving the increase in real GDP and given the renewed weakness in the manufacturing PMI, this is not about to change (Figure 8).

Inflation

<table><tr><td colspan="6">Consumer prices</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Flash Jul</td><td>Final Jul</td></tr><tr><td>Tue</td><td>Euro area (final)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Aug 20</td><td>HICP (%m/m, nsa)</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>11:00am</td><td>HICP (%m/m, sa ECB)</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>HICP (%oya, nsa)</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.5</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.6</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>HICP core (%m/m, sa ECB)</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>HICP core (%oya, nsa)</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.9</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>HICP (%m/m, ex-tob.)</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.2</td><td></td><td></td></tr></table>

Euro area core prices rose 0.30%m/m, sa, in the flash report for July. This reflected equal gains in core goods prices and services price. However, the 0.30%m/m, sa, increase in core goods prices owed a lot to Italy, where the timing or intensity of seasonal sales (especially of clothing) led to a jump in core goods inflation. We suspect that this effect is transitory. Another factor that affected the interpretation of the data is that the ECB’s seasonally adjusted data (i.e., the 0.30%m/m, sa, increase in core prices) was lower than is consistent Eurostat’s non-seasonally adjusted increase of 2.9%oya in core inflation overall. Hence, a calculation effect may have inflated the latter. Unfortunately, only the flash report for August will reveal how these effects play out. But, the final details for July will still help to gauge the underlying trend. In our view, there is some modest slowing in core inflation, with seasonally adjusted services prices rising 0.30%m/m, sa, over the past two months, compared to average gains of 0.45%m/ m, sa, in the first five months of the year. Hence, the flash report is hinting at some progress on services inflation.

The Euro area PMI disappointed significantly in July, taking its ex-Ireland GDP growth signal from 1.3%ar in May to just 0.8%ar in July. This has already prompted us to reduce our 3Q24 GDP forecast from 1.5%q/q saar to 1.0%q/q saar. Given the back-to-back declines of the PMI, the August report will be crucial in gauging where the economy is heading. Clearly, any further decline would raise broader downside risks to our forecast, which now has GDP growing at a 1%ar over the next four quarters. Our sense is that the fundamentals do support a trend-like growth forecast, but it will be important to see this validated by the August PMI. In particular, we expect the PMI to edge a bit higher, but even a stable outcome would be broadly consistent with our GDP projection. Should the PMI decline, it would be important to assess if this may be a temporary reaction to the recent repricing in financial markets and the increased macro risks in the US.

In terms of the details, the July report was particularly weak in manufacturing, whereas the recent declines have been much more modest and from much higher levels in services. The PMI employment index also still showed further hiring, even if at a somewhat reduced pace. In August, it would be reassuring to see manufacturing improve somewhat after its recent relapse and for services to stabilize. At the country level, it will be important to see some improvement in German manufacturing in particular. France may see some Olympicsrelated lift in services.

National data suggest that Euro area construction output may have increased modestly, reversing only a part of the recent declines. A part of the latter is weather-related payback, but nevertheless leaves 2Q24 looking weak overall with a decline of almost 5%q/q saar. We note however that the ECB bank lending survey is now pointing to a return to positive mortgage demand growth, which could support the sector going forward.

<table><tr><td></td><td>oonsumer comdence (prem)</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>JulAug</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Thu</td><td>Euro area (European Commission survey)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Aug 22 % balance of responses</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>4:00pm Consumer confidence</td><td>-14.3</td><td>-14.0</td><td>-13.0</td><td>-12.5</td></tr></table>

Euro area consumer confidence has continued to steadily improve in recent months, even as business confidence has moderated. In light of the solid gains in real incomes, it is likely that consumer confidence continued to improve in August. Concerns about unemployment have edged up, but views about personal finances have continued to improve and that has also lifted intentions to make major purchases. Of course, consumers may react negatively to the recent moves in financial markets and growth risks, but we suspect that confidence is still well supported at this stage.

## Japan

• 2Q GDP bounces on consumption recovery

• Expecting sustained upturn in domestic demand...

• ...giving BoJ confidence for policy normalization

• Business sentiment softens temporarily amid market volatility

• Next week: July core CPI stabilizes

Stronger-than-expected GDP growth in 2Q24 underscores Japan’s solid economic recovery, driven primarily by a rebound in domestic demand. With improving household incomes and consumer sentiment, along with robust capital expenditure, the outlook remains positive. And a recovery in domestic demand should support further rises in prices, leaving core inflation close to the BoJ’s 2% target through 2H24.

As nominal wages continue to trend higher, lifting incomes and activity, this dynamic should give the BoJ confidence to adjust its “highly accommodative” policy settings. We continue to expect the BoJ to raise the policy rate by 25bp to 0.5% in December this year.

## 2Q GDP upswing on consumption recovery

The Cabinet Office’s first estimate for 2Q24 GDP exceeded expectations (JPM: 2.0%, Consensus: 2.3%), rising 3.1%q/q, saar. The positive surprise was driven by domestic demand, which contributed 3.9%-pt to overall growth, while net external demand slightly detracted by 0.2%-pt (Figures 1 and 2). In the details:

Reflecting rising wages and the government’s one-off tax cuts, private consumption rebounded sharply by 4.0%, more than offsetting the revised 2.3% decline (previously reported as -2.9%) in 1Q (Figure 3). The 2Q rebound in GDP-based consumption was stronger than our forecasts based on monthly real consumption data. Rising nominal wages and strong summer bonus payments, combined with the government’s one-off tax cuts, appear to have supported the recent recovery in consumer sentiment. With the gradual fading of real income drags, we continue to expect consumption to recover towards the year-end, forecasting a 1.5% rise in 3Q.

Both capex and residential investment showed strong gains, rising 3.6% and 6.7%, respectively. Given the structural tightening in labor, firms continue to indicate strong capex plans. We expect fixed investment to trend higher, as ongoing labor shortages are likely to catalyze an increase in firms’ capital expenditure.

Both exports and imports of goods and services increased significantly, rising by 5.9% and 7.1%, respectively. However, the export recovery was somewhat weaker compared to the 17.2% plunge in 1Q, likely due to another round of auto factory shutdowns starting in June. We expect the export recovery still has some way to go.

## Sentiment softens amid August volatility

The Reuters Tankan survey of large manufacturers’ sentiment fell 1 point to 10 in August, after a solid 5 point rise in July. Though the level remains high relative to the first half of the year, there was a strong pullback from the much more optimistic July number in some sectors (Figure 4).

Auto producers’ sentiment fell by 14pts to 0; electronics by 10pts to -15, and the volatile oil refining sector fell 34pts to 16. Non-manufacturing sentiment also declined for the second consecutive month, dropping to 24 from 26 in July, but remains elevated from a longer-run perspective. Most of the August decline in non-manufacturers’ sentiment was due to a large drop in the information and communication sector, which fell to 36 pts from 54. By contrast, sentiment in the retail sector reversed a large decline in June, rising 9 points to 14, consistent with the more upbeat message from recent consumer spending and sentiment data (Figures 5 and 6).

Figure 4: Reuters Tankan large firms sentiment - Headline DI, level of conditions, boxes denote 3-mo outlook
![](images/f78a49a2a8541f3eb75d9c78d333e22d6b0ee66229f9f7f658e9d473e2405efa.jpg)
Source: Reuters, J.P. Morgan

Figure 5: Reuters Tankan, autos and transport equipment DI, level of conditions
![](images/40b50232a741178c19ea2a7dc52f8ae70c2d50b689b84de357709515a25eb360.jpg)

Figure 6: Reuters Tankan, retail
![](images/d3921904dc3c63a5f0435f9d4c33787f4b1192068b38a7d9e5a71b69deff8fac.jpg)
Source: Reuters, J.P. Morgan

Since the survey was conducted in early August, large market movements owing to the impact of a sharp unwind of yenfunded carry trade appear to have influenced some respondents. Survey respondents referred to uncertainty around FX rates as a concern. They also cited sluggish demand in China. Against this backdrop, and reflecting higher input cost putting pressure on margins, manufacturers’ outlook indicators fell to 5, the lowest level since February.

## Canada

• Next week’s July CPI report should support continued easing by the BoC

• We see headline CPI easing to 2.5%oya and core CPI moving down to 2.6%oya in July

Mixed signals from housing market data continue to flag a supply-demand mismatch

Disinflation in Canada hasn’t been as immaculate as it appears to be in the US: GDP growth in Canada has mostly lagged its southern neighbor for the past year and half, and we suspect 2Q growth will come in softer than the 2.8% q/q saar pace recorded for the US last quarter. In addition, the labor market has softened more notably in Canada than the US: since the start of 2023, the unemployment rate has risen 1.3%-pts in Canada versus 0.8%-pts in the US. This relative weakness is reflected in rising “excess supply” that the Bank of Canada has leaned upon to motivate their policy easing thus far, even as the inflation and wage growth data had firmed ahead of the second 25bp rate cut in late July. By contrast, next week’s July CPI report is likely to help make more of a case for continued easing by the BoC.

Specifically, we look for headline CPI to have eased to 2.5%oya in July—despite some upward pressure from energy prices—while core CPI moved down to 2.6%oya last month. We expect lower prices for durable goods to offset a pickup in prices at the pump and keep headline inflation on an easing trend in July. Price growth for goods has softened to just above zero in year-ago terms, while continued strength in shelter price growth has kept services prices firm (Figure 1). While we expect some moderation in mortgage interest costs as the BoC proceeds with its easing cycle, shelter price growth will remain elevated in July. The BoC’s preferred core measures should stay inside the target zone in July.

Figure 1: Select categories of CPI
![](images/83351c75db2e743232acbc153b936715db3d8a3b7bfa623b70a7c0ac38618bcd.jpg)

If realized, this would mark the sixth straight month with the year-ago rate for exclusionary core (excluding food and energy) inside the target zone, and such an outcome should reinforce the dovish lean by the BoC currently. We continue to look for a third consecutive 25bp cut next month as Governor Macklem has emphasized that subsequent easing will proceed gradually.

## A house divided

Canada’s housing starts rose to a 13-month high with an annualized 279,509 units in July, according to data released by the Canada Mortgage and Housing Corporation this week. Starts were up 15.7% on the month, with the July increase led by multifamily units. Indeed, multifamily units appear to be driving most of the dynamics for starts overall — in contrast to the US, where single-family start are the key component moving the total. A notable gap has opened up in Canada between multifamily starts, which have broadly remained firm, and single-family starts, which have weakened more significantly (Figure 2). The combination of high interest rates and the high indebtedness of Canadian households looks to be crimping demand for single-family units, whereas the surge in immigration meanwhile is likely helping boost demand for multifamily units.

Figure 2: Housing starts
![](images/a59fc109ff590ed111bd93b767e630f73ccd226d4992f5bbe1ad84891ef2054d.jpg)

Other housing data look to be moderating as well after a sizable boom post-pandemic. Existing home sales have settled at a level slightly below their pre-pandemic average. Recent data from the Canadian Real Estate Association show existing home sales down 0.7% in July, the fifth drop in six months. The run up in mortgage interest rates as the BoC’s hiking cycle commenced took some steam out of housing demand; mortgage rates are now starting to come back down as the BoC presses on with its easing cycle. As we’ve noted before, what the housing market needs is additional supply — but data on building permits do not point to clear signs of improvement there either. Total building permits on a constant dollar basis fell 13.9% in June. Both residential (-11.5%) and non-residential (-18.1%) permits declined in June, with a notable slump (-19.8%) in the volatile multi-unit component of the residential sector. Multi-unit residences are an important piece in addressing tight housing supply.
