## Mexico

Banxico minutes to confirm majority of members focused on reducing the tight policy stance

• Preliminary Aug inflation next week will deliver mixed news but ease concerns regarding headline

• Final 2Q24 GDP release to confirm last quarter’s growing pains

• Salient Congress in a hurry to approve AMLO reforms

The minutes from Banxico, to be released next Thursday, will be paramount to understand and calibrate the board’s sensitivity in terms of future actions, particularly considering the divided 3-2 decision to cut. We did expect the cut to be 10.75%, but in our view, even considering the hawkish dissenters, the bias of the statement was fairly dovish, which suggested the majority of the board is comfortable with the resuming rate cuts at this early stage of the ‘cycle’.

To us, the key to understand the bias and the guidance—as the statement concluded they will continue to discuss cuts in upcoming COPOM discussions—lies in the tight policy stance as explained by the ex-ante policy rate (Figure 1). Even incorporating two additional cuts this year, the (real) policy rate will remain above 6%, well above the neutral range determined by Banxico. Considering how medium- and long-term expectations have evolved and the ongoing disinflation gains—particularly the consolidation of core inflation near the upper 4% reference—grant the board space to reactivate the easing cycle and should allow further cuts this year and next.

Figure 1: Ex-ante real policy rate
![](images/dec8f0348133065d2e27d0a09f54b03f5a9aeda78ee85109277fc239f054b1c0.jpg)
Source: Banxico and J.P. Morgan forecasts. Ex-ante policy rate range is Banxico's. Outlook updated Aug 15, 2024

Further delaying rate cuts would have exerted too much pressure on economic and financial conditions, as is now evident in the ongoing economic deceleration that gained traction in 1Q24. We agree with the recent emphasis on downside risks to economic activity and expect further details on this in next week’s minutes. It is particularly concerning how the Mexican economy has lost traction with the US economy still relatively resilient. With domestic demand expected to cool further, underlying inflation pressures should continue to ease, resulting in further declines ahead.

Yet, the recent concerns related to a potential pass-through on the back of raw-food spikes and FX depreciation should also be taken seriously, even if it is too early to incorporate significant adjustments into the CPI projections. To be sure, the board is not talking about rate reductions that would bring the real policy rate out of the restrictive zone (i.e. above 3.4%) any time soon and gradual 25bp actions are welcome and warranted in the context of the current balance of risks to inflation (upside) and growth (downside).

## No upsets expected for 1H August inflation

The advance inflation report for August, also to be released next week, is expected to keep the balance of risks relatively unchanged. Non-core inflation will likely deliver mixed results, with some key staples probably easing (tomatoes, onions) and others exerting upward pressures (energy, meat, and eggs).

Still, biweekly prints should not alter recent discussion, with annual headline inflation easing some, to 5.3% from 5.5% in July on the back of a 0.14%2w/2w increase. This should leave inflation on track to reach 5.2% at the end of 3Q and 4.6% by the end of the year. Still the positive news remains on the core front, with the annual reading expected to slip to 4.03% from 4.08%. Both core goods and services will evidence some seasonal dynamics, with school fares and nonfood goods up and airfares and tourism down. But stripping out the seasonal factors, we forecast core inflation stable at its pre-pandemic average, as it has been the case since May (Figure 2).

![](images/2b03bdbf9753d3ef98282db2d9f3f130cf9dcd42df8a3334eced41ec11c66402.jpg)

For the case of non-core inflation, we believe it is too early to consider the current spike to be permanent even if we continue to incorporate a number of upside surprises related to the wild mood swings in weather conditions and more recently market frictions (Figure 3). Still, we expect a mean reversal in raw-food prices in the next prints considering welcome news in terms of agricultural supply—except for lime prices, which are expected to increase sharply in 2H Aug.

![](images/f40bda207a361ad4890e344a06b3c581ac5c206a558554b72268e38ef8fec35e.jpg)

## June GDP a modest positive

After the release of the industrial production report for June, we noted modest upside risks to economic activity in 2Q, which according to the Flash report in July, came in at 0.9%saar, well below our long-awaited bounce that never materialized. On the heels of the 0.4%samr for IP June— mostly rooted in a strong bounce of 2% in manufacturing— and an expected 2% gain in primary activity we expect a modest 0.1% increase in June GDP (Figure 4), which is consistent with a marginal upward revision for 2Q to 1.2%saar. This would not alter our updated 1.3%y/y full-year forecast.

![](images/6482d5ebdc578f71b488b067d3510ba070fe55b8c99eed05a330d6fcd4142c8d.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan. June data is forecast

## Constitutional reforms gain traction

As the old congress prepares to complete its term on August 31, law-makers from the ruling Morena coalition in the Permanent Commission (the full congress is in Summer break) are unusually busy fine-tuning the reforms agenda prepared by President Lopez (known as AMLO). While these constitutional reforms will need to be voted by the new congress once it is sworn in early September, by discussing and polishing the drafts (Dictamenes, in Spanish) now, the voting process is expected to be relatively fast once the Morena super-majority machine arrives on September 1.

On Wednesday, the Permanent Commission approved the Dictamen for the energy sector reforms, reviving the electricity bill proposed, passed, but eventually blocked by the Supreme Court in 2022. There was an extended discussion in terms of strategic sectors and why the need to avoid major private investment. Lithium exploration was granted strategic status, while open-pit mining and internet services were also in the bill.

The draft also stated that a 'state monopoly' cannot be called a 'monopoly' given its strategic objectives and social priorities for the administration. Last week, reform drafts for granting constitutional status to key social programs and the annual minimum wage increases (in real terms) were also passed, and next week, the eagerly awaited Judiciary Reform Dictamen will be discussed, on August 22.

In our view, these developments are outright negative given Morena’s urgency to prepare reforms without consultations with the private sector. Yet, we will have to await next week’s details on the Judiciary bill and for September to confirm how uneven the legislative field is.

## Brazil

BCB board’s hawkish rhetoric remains in evidence

• FX appreciation can reduce pressure for projections...

• ...but data flow reinforces risks to our stable Selic call

• 2024 hikes wouldn’t lessen our conviction on 2025 cuts

Over the past two weeks BRL went from testing its weakest levels since 2022 to appreciating over 5% and edging closer to USDBRL5.45 (Figure 1). The move happened amid a busy period, when hawkish COPOM minutes were followed by cautious remarks from different board members, reinforcing the market pricing of hikes as soon as September and contrasting with our call for stable rates until mid-2025.

Figure 1: Brazilian real exchange rate
![](images/ea6af9cbddee17ca1bd26dd5dc79f2c2503671bd070bb45c9c23eaec1994c52d.jpg)

Meanwhile several indicators revealed surprisingly strong growth over 2Q, which is even more impressive given the disruption from May’s floods in RS. Despite weaker retail sales, both industrial production and services performed better than expected in June, increasing the distance between our initial GDP forecast for 2Q and our nowcaster, leading us to update said forecast to 4.8% saar (Figure 2).

Figure 2: GDP forecast revision index
![](images/8f2f5de4394451c1b1c33731b5874b3433c84d2604cc94cc5d6be9529d676054.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

The new path translates into an upward revision of four tenths to our 2024 GDP growth to 2.9%—crowning yet another year when reality trumps our initial forecast, even as we started out with a more constructive view. Stronger activity and higher inflation, as shown by our decision to increase our CPI

forecast by 20bp to 4.2% for 2024, would argue in favor of more caution, which in a context of a tight labor market and de-anchored expectations may very well translate into higher rates. There are two main reasons why we think they should not. One is that more front-loaded cuts in the US should make room for a different discussion in EM as well, a move that should be kicked-off mere hours before the next COPOM meeting. Another is that keeping rates stable for longer should be enough to bring BCB’s inflation forecasts to levels closer to the 3.0% target for the relevant horizon—particularly if the BRL appreciates further until September 18.

Our conviction on rate stability in 2024 is lower now than in the past, as we acknowledge that the board may opt to hike in a bid to front-load the convergence—potentially gaining some credibility in the process. Still, this discussion does not change our stance that Selic will go down in 2025. Rather, in our view, eventual hikes this year would reinforce the space for cuts next year. Coupled with the external outlook, the fiscal stance ranks as an important risk to this call. Though concerned with the direction that some discussions on states’ debt renegotiation and payroll tax breaks are taking in Congress, we remain of the view that the August 31 budget proposal is the key event to monitor. We expect it to abide by the existing target and framework, and caution that anything short of that could sour the mood.

## Argentina

Headline CPI increased 4.0%m/m in July (263.4%oya and 87% YTD), in line with our expectations

Core CPI again proved sticky to the downside at 3.8%m/m, with core ex-food at 4.5%m/m

• Lower regulated price increases explained the monthly headline deceleration

Releasing capital controls is a necessary condition to ensure a sustainable core CPI deceleration ahead

## Core inflation proved again sticky

Headline CPI increased 4.0%m/m in July (263.4%oya and 87% YTD), in line with our expectations. The monthly inflation print thus resumed decelerating after the slight rebound reported the prior month, on the back of regulated prices adjustments. Seasonally adjusted (by J.P. Morgan), national headline CPI gained 4.7%m/m, sa, with the last-three-month sequential pace easing to 88.8%saar.

As anticipated, core CPI proved again sticky to the downside at 3.8%m/m in July, relatively in line with the prior month’s result (3.7%m/m), yet below the 4.6% monthly average in the prior three months (Figure 1). Meanwhile, our preferred metric of underlying inflation (adjusting core inflation by excluding food prices) printed at 4.5%m/m, compared to 5.0%m/m in the prior 3-month average. Worth noting, ex. food core momentum is running at 75.2% in annualized terms (sa), decelerating from 91.6% in June.

By component, food inflation accelerated a bit on the month to 3.2%m/m (+1.0%-pt contribution), from the 3.0% low observed in June. Adding granularity, fresh food (mainly vegetables) re-accelerated, with that contribution increasing to 54bp from 37bp the prior month. On the other side, housing and basic services eased to 6%m/m (+0.5%-pt contribution) from June’s double-digit print.

As noted above, the monthly headline inflation deceleration was fully explained by lower regulated price increases, with the contribution declining by 0.7%-pt since the June tariff adjustments. Meanwhile, seasonal prices accelerated to 5.1%m/m (+0.6%-pt contribution), from 4.4%m/m (0.5%-pt) in June (Figure 2).

Figure 1: Headline and core National-CPI
![](images/0334b77e5c1b892dcfa59bc08a896f1758e549128432e9e992b730ee4588e893.jpg)
Figure 2: National-CPI

%-pt, contribution to headline CPI
![](images/7b0fe7ea721269551098736286bef6598d3ecd1f9b131632bfea398e3dac39d2.jpg)
Source: INDEC and J.P. Morgan

## Further regulated price increases

New regulated price adjustments were confirmed for August. The government announced electricity and gas tariffs will increase by around 4% on average in the month, which adds to the scheduled increase in the prices of fuels, water, and bus fares in AMBA. In our estimates, the announced corrections jointly contribute 0.9%-pt to headline CPI. Against this backdrop, we see headline inflation proving sticky around a 4% monthly level in August. While the future pace of regulated price adjustments remains uncertain, we estimate around 6%-pts in additional contribution to headline CPI coming from increases in electricity, gas, fuels, and transportation prices.

Against this backdrop, we see 3Q24 inflation averaging 4.3%m/m, with a modest rebound anticipated in 4Q24 (5% monthly average), assuming the transition to a new policy framework by year-end. This is consistent with a year-end 2024 inflation forecast at 135%oya. Worth noting, we see monthly core inflation averaging 3.3% in 2H24 (Figure 3), a level that seems difficult to break absent further amendments to the policy framework. We view advancing toward promptly reducing the (still large) relative price misalignment and releasing capital controls as two necessary conditions to ensure a sustainable disinflation path ahead.

Figure 3: Headline CPI
![](images/f6cdcd0ec8c21a64b625d5b211d36f921b24a4334af8f445102b91694a70cced.jpg)

## United Kingdom

• Labour market still looks resilient

• Inflation undershoots but underlying still provides grounds for caution

GDP strong in 2Q and we revise up 3Q to 1.5% annualized

• BoE to continue easing but at a slow quarterly pace; new MPC member appointed

The data this week should offer the BoE a little encouragement in the soft landing view but with plenty of reason to proceed cautiously. Growth is running firm and the labour market still looks resilient. We await next week’s PMI for August, but the July retail sales data this week suggest consumer spending is on a solid footing to show a continued recovery in 3Q. We revised up our 3Q GDP forecast by 0.5%-pts to 1.5%, ar this week. Notwithstanding a softer underlying services inflation print this week, the run rate remains firm and the BoE has good reason to remain cautious on rates. We expect it to continue easing at a quarterly pace, with the next move in November, but see no urgency for it to cut again at the very next September meeting.

Separately, the Chancellor confirmed this week that Alan Taylor will replace outgoing MPC member Jonathan Haskel on an initial three year term. Taylor is an economist who is currently Professor of International and Public Affairs at Columbia University, New York. He has served as a senior advisor to major financial institutions including Morgan Stanley and PIMCO, is a visiting scholar at the Bank of England and has published papers in the fields of macroeconomics, international economics, finance and economic history. Its always hard to know which way newly appointed members will lean, but we did find this paper from May 2023 interesting where Taylor questions the idea that monetary policy is neutral in the long run. He finds evidence that tight monetary can have long term adverse impacts on output, particularly on capital and total factor productivity growth. This is an area where the UK has been underperforming for some time. He finds this impact to be asymmetric, with unusually loose policy failing to have a positive impact.

## Labour market still looks resilient

The latest labour market report again showed resilience. Following a five-month rising trend to 4.4%, the ILO unemployment rate dropped to 4.2% in the three months to June. The household survey helped the decline, showing a recovery in employment growth (+1.2%, 3m/3m, saar) and the participation rate ticking down a tenth. The move down in the unemployment rate is large for a single month, but the move should not be seen as a great surprise: due to measurement problems the BoE has recently downplayed the prior rise and described the underlying trend as close to flat. The data appear to vindicate that view amid considerable monthly noise.

The alternative payroll estimate of employee jobs showed a small 24k gain over the month to July, but this measure is up at a healthy 1% annualized pace over the past three months. Vacancies fell but just about remain above pre-COVID levels. One eye-catching move was the large rise in the claimant count unemployment rate from 4.4% to 4.7% in July. This has been a relatively stable series for some time. According to the ONS, however, this rise relates to changes in eligibility requirements implemented by the Department for Work and Pensions in May. This is likely to affect 180,000 people over a six-month period, equivalent to 0.5% of the population. Since May the claimant count has risen by 0.4%-pts.

Pay was broadly as expected, with non-bonus average weekly earnings falling from an upwardly revised 5.8% to 5.4% (oya, 3mma) for June. The same was true in the private sector, where earnings fell from 5.6% to 5.2%. Nevertheless, this metric was a tick above the BoE’s 5.1% forecast. These outturns translate into a still strong 0.4% gain over the month. There is a National Living Wage impact on the data, but the 3m/3m, annualized rate is running up at 7.2%. This should fall closer to 5% in the next couple of releases, but that pace is still very strong. Overall, we see little in the report that should add any urgency for the BoE.

## Inflation undershoots in July

Headline CPI inflation for July rose from 2.0% to 2.2%oya (JPMorgan & Consensus 2.3; BoE 2.4). Core inflation fell from 3.5% to 3.3% (JPMorgan 3.4 & Consensus 3.4). Services inflation fell from 5.7% to 5.2% (JPMorgan & Consensus 5.5; BoE 5.6). RPI was up from 2.9% to 3.6%oya (JPMorgan 3.7; Consensus 3.5).

Based on our seasonal adjustment, we estimate that headline CPI prices were flat over the month. That largely reflected an anticipated 7% drop in regulated domestic energy bills, with core prices rising 0.3% over the month. That was a touch lower than expected. The bulk of the downside surprise came from services which rose 0.3%, the softest gain since February. There was some offset to this from a 0.2% gain in core goods reflecting audio-visuals and clothing.

Ahead of this release there was uncertainty about whether transport services in July would reproduce last year’s large 3.7% gain, as well as the extent to which package holidays and accommodation services would unwind last month’s 3.2% spike (which appeared to have some Taylor Swift influence). In the event both of these factors were the source of the downside surprise in July. Transport services (which includes airfares) fell 1% over the month while there was an almost complete 2.8% unwind in the accommodation category. This is unusual as prices in this category seldom fall and tend only to unwind part of their prior spike. This observation itself also points to some impact from the concerts, and the ONS noted that these prices fell “particularly for single 1 night stay hotel stays with breakfast”.

The impact of the accommodation surprise (vs. our forecast) on the headline CPI was still contained at -0.14%, but the near 50% weight of services means the influence on services was closer to -0.3%. If we include the airfares surprise, the total impact of these two surprises combined was worth around -0.2% on the headline CPI and 0.4% on services. The reason services didn’t come in even lower is due to a 1.4% spike in rents, and to some extent a 1% gain in miscellaneous services. The large rise in rents came from a 4.3% surge in non-private rental prices.

The BoE has typically set aside these more volatile categories when assessing underlying inflation. As such, its persistence weighted measure of services rose by 0.3%, sa in July, firm overall but cooler than in prior months on average. On a 3m/3m annualized basis this metric, previously highlighted by the BoE, is running at 5.6%. That is strong but still a cooling from a recent spike of over 6%.

While the services surprise is particularly large versus the BoE’s forecast, the policy implications of this should be more limited. Although the BoE has recently discouraged a focus on specific metrics in the data, it is still looking at the risks associated from inflation persistence and so the persistence weighted CPI services measure clearly still has value. While the run rate on core services is still firm, there are some hints of progress and this should make the BoE feel better about the risks and general direction of travel. We note the services CPI data are now returning to the moderating path laid out by the PMI prices component (Figure 1).

There is still a lot of data to go before the next BoE meeting, including another CPI report. So far we see little in the latest domestic data that should create greater urgency around the pace of future BoE easing. With the underlying pace of inflation remaining strong, and the drag from energy set to reduce further in 2H24, we expect the headline CPI to rise to around 2.7% by year-end with core over 3%. This should limit the pace of easing. But the BoE should at least feel comfortable in cutting gradually, and we look for the next cut to come in November.

## 2Q GDP rose 0.6%, we nudge 3Q up a little

GDP rose 0.6%q/q in 2Q (2.3% annualized), in line with our and the consensus expectation although a touch below the BoE’s 0.7% estimate. It had felt like the consumer was starting to get going lately but the reported 0.2% gain in spending in this report was a clear disappointment. Likewise, despite robust confidence, business investment contracted by 0.1%. Government consumption instead did the heavy lifting with a 1.4% gain. The rest of the demand side was awash with noise and statistical caveats. Net trade was recorded as a large drag on growth but this was fully offset by the shifts in non-monetary gold and inventories (which includes the alignment adjustment).

Nominal GDP rose 0.9%q/q, with the deflator up by just 0.3%q/q. That leaves the deflator up by only 2.2% compared to a year ago. While some measures of underlying domestic inflation (e.g. the services CPI and average earnings) continue to run strong, this broader economy-wide measure continues to show a steady normalization and paints a much more benign picture.

The demand composition described above suggests underlying GDP growth is softer than the headline figure, although we note that these data do get heavily revised. This was the case with 1Q, when the initial consumer picture (also a 0.2%q/q) ended up looking much better after revisions (a 0.4%). We continue to see positive fundamentals due to growth in real incomes and rising household confidence. The PMI rose in July and expected home sales picked up.

Monthly GDP turned out flat in June, although this came after a strong 0.4%m/m in the prior month and was a slightly better outturn than in our forecast. That leaves June GDP 0.1% higher than its 2Q average. Services fell 0.1% over the month, due to payback from outsized gains in May within the distribution, accommodation and food services sectors. But this was offset by solid gains of 0.8% and 0.5% in IP and construction respectively (manufacturing was up 1.1% in June). Taking the latest data into account, we are lifting our 3Q GDP forecast from 0.3% to 0.4%q/q (from 1.0% to 1.5% annualized).

Retail sales rose 0.5%m/m, sa in July, reversing part of the prior month’s 0.9% drop (which was revised to show a smaller decline). More importantly, the level of retail spending in July is 0.7% above its 2Q average owing to the strength seen back in May which was not fully unwound. This sets 3Q on a solid footing and suggests the awakening of the consumer in 1H24 is continuing into 3Q. We revise up our 3Q GDP forecast this week. We will evaluate that revision after next week’s PMI for August but the latest retail sales data support the change.

## Emerging Europe

Czech Republic: Stronger than expected inflation in July

• Romania: Inflation spike on non-core

• Romania: Revising GDP growth lower

• Poland: Bright spot of CEE growth

## Czech Republic: Stronger than expected inflation in July

Inflation turned out a bit stronger than expected in July, with consumer prices up 0.7% m/m (0.5% expected), leading to 2.2%oya (Figure 1), from 2% (Consensus and J.P. Morgan: 2.0%oya). The noisy elements which triggered the last couple of months’ downside surprises, food and also importantly package holidays (defying the usual seasonal pattern), have now accelerated beyond expectations. Beyond the noise, not much has changed: inflation is still close to target at the headline level, and trending in underlying terms at around 2.4-2.5% saar pace.

Food prices printed much stronger than expected, up 0.3%m m, whereas we predicted a 0.9% decline based on the midmonth surveys. The unexpected price rise occurs despite a decline of 2.2%m/m in the price of fruits and a 4.6% drop in the case of vegetables. The biggest positive contributions came from meat prices (1.8%m/m) and confectionery (sugar, jam, honey etc., up 3.5%). In over-year ago terms food inflation turned less negative to 3.1%oya, from -4.2%, still one of the lowest in Europe. Alcohol and tobacco prices were unchanged and provided some offset to food’s stronger momentum (we expected 0.4%m/m in a&t). Fuel prices were up 1.3%m/m, roughly as indicated in the weekly surveys, but because of a favorable base fell to 3%oya, from 6.2%. Regulated prices were roughly unchanged.

Core inflation printed 1.1%m/m, leading to an increase to 2.3%oya, from 2.2%, in both cases one decimal stronger than our forecast (and the CNB’s). The alternative core measure which we track, excluding rents and package holidays was up 0.26%m/m. Compared to the historical pattern for the month of July, both core measures show that underlying inflation has dropped substantially from the highs of 2021-22, but are still running above the pre-Covid trend.

We estimate that in seasonally-adjusted terms, core CPI was up 0.33%m/m in July, or 4.1% in annualized terms, a rather high reading which we believe however, is not a good representation of the underlying pace. July’s chunky is preceded by two rather low prints (-1.1% sa in June and 1.3% in May), which we have argued are driven by excess volatility in the series due to shifts in the usual seasonality of package holi-

days (lower than usual in May/June and now rebounding beyond the norm in July). Because of that we have attached more weight to our core ex. rents and holidays as a better measure of underlying dynamics, and indeed we see (Figure 2) that the noise in core tends to oscillate around our alternative series. In that metric, core printed a 2.6% saar in July, and is trending around 2.4-2.5% over the past few months.

Everything from the previous uber-hawkish CNB meeting suggested the board has now less confidence about further easing, entering now a fine-tuning stage in which it will proceed in tentative steps of 25bps. This week’s print, a bit stronger than the central bank’s forecast, but not decisively so (core 2.3% vs 2.2% expected), does not make a big difference in our view. We expect two additional 25bp rate cuts this year, in September and November, bringing rates to 4%. We predict inflation will be above target, but sub 3%oya on trend, with core CPI averaging 2.4% in 2H24 and 2.5% in 2025. If inflation were to surprise us to the downside, trending at target for example, we think the CNB would cut a bit more than our base case, by another 50bps or so.

## Romania: Inflation spike on non-core

Headline inflation jumped to 5.4%oya in July from 4.9% in June. On non-core items - we expected a smaller increase to 5%oya (sequential pace was 0.6%m/m). A large increase in fuel prices on the back of excise adjustment was the main inflationary force as it added 0.3%pts to the sequential pace.

But that is not the surprise for us as fuel inflation forecast was very close to actual. Other non-core items drove the surprise. Volatile food surprised on the upside across all three components: vegetables, fruit and egg; the former alone explains a tenth of the error at the level of the headline. In addition, the upward surprises in non-food goods category were driven by 1.1%m/m in each of electricity and natural gas prices. The cumulated impact of these two explains another one tenth of the error at the level of the headline. Looking at over-yearago rates of inflation, both food and non-food reversed the downtrend (Figure 3). In both cases, non-core components are to blame: volatile food as well as energy (fuel, electricity and natural gas).

Even though headline CPI was stronger than expected, core inflation was in line with the forecast at 0.3%m/m and 5.5%oya (from 5.7% in June). We see this as a small positive because core has been at 0.3%m/m for the past three months relative to 0.6%m/m on average over January-April. Marketbased core goods and core services were also largely as expected.

We are only marginally encouraged by positive core developments in recent months, because once data are seasonally adjusted the picture is less constructive. The July print was in fact rather strong with momentum at about 5.5% saar from 3.5%-4.5% in prior two months. The acceleration in July keeps almost flat the 3-month pace at about 5% saar. If we decompose core into processed food and market-based goods and services, the 3m saar momentum flattening is driven by the processed food component (Figure 4). This is likely to remain problematic as core food momentum is rather low; in best case, stability at low levels could materialize.

Core goods are finally showing better behaviour with a sharp fall in momentum in both June and July after very high pace; we expected a reversal though it came a bit late. Likely momentum in goods will stay low or could decline further. Core services slowed rather sharply over May-June (to a 5-6% saar pace) and July marks a correction to about 8%. It remains to be seen if this is transitory or not - overall, we would expect more persistence in core services.

As we highlighted in our note last week, we see room for NBR to be surprised higher on core inflation as it sharply revised lower the profile. The bank only publishes end-quarter forecasts and, for core, we have 5.5% in September versus 5.3% expected by the NBR. The acceleration in headline also appears to be an upside surprise for the NBR. Even with disinflation in August and September, we see only 4.5% to be reached relative to 4.2% expected by the NBR. And risks to both core and headline are to the upside in our forecast. Taking into account the complicated fiscal and political environment, we think the NBR Board will not cut the key rate for the rest of 2024.

## Romania: Revising GDP growth lower

Romania’s GDP growth can be characterized as the result of some sort of random-numbers generating procedure. This is because of the disconnect between reported GDP growth and main components such as IP, retail sales and construction for which high-frequency data exist (Figure 5). For this reason, we take the numbers released this week with a pinch of salt. Revisions to historical data, sometimes sizeable revisions, are certainly possible (Figure 6), but large changes for 2024 are possible only in spring 2025.

Having said that, and because of the slowing in over-year-ago numbers (and assuming these won’t be revised significantly), it is undeniable that GDP growth slowed markedly in Romania in 2Q24, in contrast to our and central bank expectations for a sizeable rebound. The flash GDP release showed marginal expansion in 2Q24 - only +0.1% relative to 1Q24 - and sharp slowing in over-year-ago to 0.7% from 2.1% in 1Q24. We expected GDP growth to be 1.5%q/q (sa) and 2.6%oya. As this is a flash release, we do not have details. However, same as other quarters, there is a contrast between what Retail sales and construction suggested relative to the flash release. It would suggest that, besides industry, other parts of the economy contracted sharply in 2Q24 to offset gains in retail and construction. With wage growth strong in nominal and

real terms and with budget deficit expanding fast, it is hard to understand what drove such a contraction in parts of the economy which are not tracked.

In the statement after the last rate meeting, the NBR said that “the latest data and analyses point to a somewhat more robust quarter-on-quarter economic growth in 2024 Q2 than previously anticipated, implying a marked step-up in the annual GDP dynamics.” As the output gap was revised lower after 1Q24 disappointed, the 2Q24 number should further compress the positive output gap.

Based on 2Q24 we revise GDP growth again to the downside (we had 4% for 2024 at the start of this year and were surprised lower in both 1Q and 2Q24). We now look for GDP growth at only 2% in 2024 compared to 3.1% previously (Table 1). Because we keep a strong rebound during 2H24 - on the view that investment has to rebound and elections-induced growth - there remain risks to the downside relative to our forecast. Even 2% is better than either Czechia or Hungary (and both had negative growth in 2023), but it would be a disappointment for Romania, particularly because fiscal expansion continued as we forecast a budget deficit of 7.4% of GDP in 2024 from 6.6% in 2023. We are also assuming negative impact in 2025 because of the fiscal consolidation. Previously we assumed full impact materializing in 1Q25, but now have split that into measures likely to impact both 1Q25 and 3Q25 (tax hikes, like VAT, are more likely in July than January 2025).

## Poland: Bright spot of CEE growth

Amidst a series of downside surprises (Czech Republic and Hungary a few weeks back, Romania this week), Poland’s 2Q24 flash national accounts release stands out as the brightest spot in the region. According to the flash release, the Polish economy grew 1.5%q/q sa in 2Q24 (6.1%q/q saar), markedly above our expectations for 1% growth (J.P. Morgan and Consensus). In addition, there were upward revisions to 1Q24 (up to 0.3%-pts to 0.8%q/q) as well as to 4Q23 and 3Q23 (+0.2%-pts and +0.4%-pts, respectively) (Figure 7). The more stable, and less frequently revised over-year-ago rate shows the economy expanding 3.2%oya (nsa), accelerating from 2% in the previous quarter. If seasonally adjusted, the pickup is even stronger, to 4%oya, from 1.8% (Figure 8). Despite the 2022-23 slowdown, the Polish economy is almost 15% larger than its pre-pandemic level, one of the best performances in Europe.

The flash release does not include a breakdown (only with the second release on August 29) and as usual the statistical office discloses absolutely no detail in the press release. Monthly activity data has become a less reliant tracker of sequential growth in recent years, but for what it’s worth, the data show a rebound in industry of 1.7%q/q in 2Q24, offset by a drop in retail sales of 0.5% (down 1.6%q/q if excluding cars) and construction fell further by 0.7%q/q after a disastrous 1Q24 where output collapsed more than 10%. Very likely we think, consumption performed overall better than suggested by retail sales (which capture goods sales only), given the strong real income growth of households. The same applies for other private services over which we have no visibility in high frequency.

Given the upside surprise as well as the upward revisions to the previous three quarters, the GDP path is mechanically lifted, even if we adjust 3Q24 somewhat lower. We now expect full year 2024 GDP growth of 3.1% (from 2.8%) and continue to project an acceleration to 3.6% in 2025.

We expect the CBRT to keep its policy rate on hold at 50%. The economic activity is slowing rapidly, but monthly inflation is likely to be above 2% in August and September due to the natural gas price and education price hikes. We expect the

CBRT to start its rate cut cycle with 250bp in November.

## South Africa

Inflation probably dropped to 4.8% in July, from 5.1%

• Downward drift in core inflation likely continues

• Our base case is for a 25bp cut in September, but with scope to do more

Economic activity data in SA have remained mixed, while the headline inflation outlook continues to improve and should see inflation temporarily drop well below the 4.5% target from September through to year-end. We expect inflation to average 4% in 4Q24, after a likely 4.6% in 3Q24 and 5.2% last quarter (Figure 1). We expect the July inflation report to underscore the incrementally improving outlook as inflation probably decelerates further to 4.8%oya, from 5.1%, helped by a gasoline price cut at the pump. Core inflation perhaps also drifted a tenth lower to 4.4%oya, hinging on car prices and public transportation costs. The main survey in July is the annual electricity tariff hike, which we expect at 11.3%m/m compared to the 14.2% last July.

Figure 1: South Africa inflation outlook
![](images/5c9c73e69ecf7ec40e3ee309238a1a4ae9dd22ccfde13ea6f316416f95d0f658.jpg)

We think that the risk distribution around the inflation outlook has become more balanced, yet uncertainty remains relatively high. True, most of the risk scenarios tie to the global backdrop and also the outlook for the terms-of-trade, while domestic risks (including political risks) have eased. The SARB may draw some comfort from a recent 2% strengthening in the rand (compared to the July MPC input assumptions), yet the currency probably remains the main upside inflation risk for the MPC. Upside inflation risks from services inflation are moderating, we think, as should be evident in the July inflation report.

The main economic developments over the next few months are the implementation of the two-pot pension reform on 1 September and the MTBPS (likely in late October). We expect a boost to household spending over the next three quarters from the access to the seed capital (capped at R30’000 per person), with much of the spending uplift concentrated in 1H15 before the impact on consumption growth and GDP growth peters out. The impact is already incorporated into our 1% GDP projection for this year and may add some concern for the MPC on upward inflationary pressures from what has so far been a very subdued consumer environment.

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australian wages grew 0.8%q/q in 2Q, a touch lower than consensus expectations

• Australia’s unemployment rate ticked up a tenth to 4.2%

• The RBNZ cut 25bp in the August meeting, the OCR track implies further easing to come

• We expect NZ retail volumes to decline 1.2%q/q in 2Q

Australian data this week confirmed the labor market continues to gradually soften. June quarter wage growth came in at 0.8%q/q, a tenth below consensus but in line with the J.P. Morgan forecast. 1Q was revised up a tenth to 0.9%q/q, leaving the annual rate at 4.1%oya.

The composition of the release was very close to our forecast with individual wage arrangements and collective agreements contributing a similar sized impulse to the headline print. As noted last week, lags in the wage setting process mean collective agreement wage growth is likely to outperform in the near term, while the contribution from individual arrangements should slow as unemployment increases (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Australian unemployment and wage growth
![](images/0fdc39a154accd420e35ab146701fe71ef06baccf9dbcfe4e492a6c5607236ad.jpg)

Award/minimum wages are reset annually from July 1 so will influence the upcoming 3Q data, but as flagged previously we expect the impulse will be negligible given the legislated increase for FY2025 was materially smaller than in previous years. In net terms, we retain the view that aggregate wage growth has peaked and forecast the annual rate to fall below 4%oya by year-end.

Wage growth remains strongest in the public sector (0.9%q/ q), particularly those industries where wages are predominantly set via collective agreements (healthcare, education and utilities). In contrast, private-sector wages (0.7%q/q) have slowed with industries reliant on individual wage arrangements such as professional/scientific, finance and realty now reporting annual wage growth below the economywide average.

## Labor market easing continues

Australia’s unemployment rate increased one tenth to 4.2% in July (J.P. Morgan/consensus: 4.1%), the highest level since early 2022 and 0.7%-pt above the cycle low (June 2023). With that in mind, the jobless rate remains a full percentage point below the immediate pre-COVID level and still low in the longer-run range, indicating the moderation in labour demand has, so far, played out in an orderly and gradual fashion.

Figure 2: Unemployment rate and NAB capacity survey utilisation
![](images/1daabefc76507229b4a0c5c807408cc3d14f696bd0c1126f5e948e555bc3b176.jpg)

The softening in the labour data is consistent with the leading labour indicators such as ANZ job advertisements and the NAB survey’s capacity utilization index (Figure 2) which have flagged deterioration for some time. Signs of cooling labour demand are also apparent in the wages data with private sector wage growth increasing 0.7%q/q in the June quarter, below the aggregate print (0.8%q/q) and a step-down from the 2023 pace. We anticipate further weakness ahead with the jobless rate to reach the mid 4s by early/mid 2025.

## RBA minutes to maintain hawkish tune

The policy Statement accompanying the RBA’s on hold decision underplayed recent concerns around the US/global backdrop, while commentary in the press conference skewed hawkish with Governor Bullock noting that “a near-term reduction in the cash rate doesn’t align with the board’s current thinking”. We expect the minutes to highlight similar themes, consistent with our view for the cash rate to remain at 4.35% through 2024.

In the press conference Bullock noted the Board discussed merits of hiking versus holding, with the minutes to provide a more detailed analysis of factors underpinning the decision. The inflation discussion is expected to dominate with guidance likely unchanged from prior iterations, noting “returning inflation to target remains the Board’s highest priority and it will do what is necessary to achieve that outcome”.

## RBNZ kicks off the easing cycle

In NZ, the RBNZ commenced the easing cycle this week, cutting the OCR 25bp to 5.25%. The first cut was delivered a year earlier than the Bank indicated in the May forecast round and against consensus expectations, although in line with our forecast.

In the press conference, Governor Orr faced numerous questions around the magnitude of the jump forward in the decision and in OCR projections vs. the surprisingly hawkish May MPS, particularly in the context of relatively unchanged 2025/26 inflation forecasts. These sorts of shifts do risk diluting the credibility of the current guidance for further cuts, though we think the RBNZ is committed to easing financial conditions and so will likely lean against market pricing if necessary over time.

In terms of why now, the activity data have dropped another gear and added urgency to shift toward neutral. The staff forecasts now project a triple-dip recession to resume in 2Q/3Q (i.e. now). The 3Q24 CPI forecast is lower by 0.7%-pt, to show inflation near the target mid-point in the current quarter, while the cash rate is still >200bp above the bank’s neutral estimates.

The statement noted "the pace of further easing will depend on the committee's confidence that pricing behaviour remains consistent with a low inflation environment, and that inflation expectations are anchored around the 2 percent target." That conveys some data dependence, though growth is weak and there is quite a buffer on the pricing indicators here, given very benign recent readings. We continue to expect 25bp moves each meeting until 2Q25, and have an end-25 cash rate of 3.5%. As discussed below, the very restrictive level of the cash rate underpins this view. The new staff forecasts show a similar path, with a trough of 3% at the terminal point of the projections.

## Greater China

• China: Credit disappointed again, led by weak loans

• Activity remained soft as policy effect is still absent

• Revising 2H quarterly growth profile

• Taiwan: 2Q GDP shows moderate consumption and export growth, with a capex bounce

• Expect steady and broad-based 2H growth

• Next week: China LPRs, Hong Kong CPI, Taiwan export orders, IP and unemployment rate

Credit data came in below expectations in July. TSF flow printed at 771bn yuan and TSF growth edged up to 8.2%oya (Figure 1). New loan creation came in weak at 260bn yuan, with loan growth slowing to 8.7%oya. Meanwhile, M2 growth edged up to 6.3% but M1 growth registered another record low at -6.6%oya. Our estimate of the credit impulse (the gap between TSF growth and nominal GDP growth) was unchanged at 4.0%pts.

Figure 1: China TSF growth
![](images/9395a75a7d836583397cb5792613e425513aace47201207f5dac3edef8e764ab.jpg)

The CNY loan component in TSF registered the first monthly decline on record (-77bn yuan), reflecting weak loan demand. Private corporates in general have remained cautious about investment decisions and increasing leverage, amid the challenging business environment, deflationary concerns, and an uncertain economic outlook. Household loans are also weak given July’s renewed weakness in new home sales. We also think banks have less incentive to lend, given low interest rates, NIM compression pressure and capital adequacy problems (especially for smaller banks). Non-loan components in TSF came in as expected in July, with net government bond issuance at 691bn yuan, net corporate bond issuance at 203bn yuan and net decline in shadow credit of 76bn yuan.

Our forecasts look for another 25bp RRR cut in 3Q and a 10bp rate cut in 4Q. The changing Fed rate outlook and mitigated CNY depreciation pressure open room for the PBOC to maintain small-step rate cuts in the coming quarters. But overall, monetary policy can hardly be labelled as accommodative given the slow pace of rate cuts and weak credit

growth. Despite the policy shift since the July Politburo meeting, the magnitude of policy adjustment runs the risk of being insufficient to stabilize the economy.

## Activity remained soft in July

China’s July activity data generally came in on the weak side, with significant slowing in exports, slowing FAI and ongoing housing drag, while domestic consumption picked up moderately (after the contraction in June). IP grew 5.1%oya, remaining flat m/m sa (0.0%), following a 1.1% m/m sa rise in June (Figure 2). Retail sales grew 2.7%oya (rising 0.8% m/ m sa, following the decline of 1.3% m/m sa in June). Service production growth came in at 4.8%oya (vs. 4.7%oya in June). Regarding the labor market, the survey-based unemployment rate rose 0.2%-pt to 5.2% in July.

Figure 2: China industrial production
![](images/2fbacdc80f4f47484ab09bd1995d4a27df8327d2c98b4f369a1a9dd7b502295f.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

Fixed investment came in below expectations, rising 3.6%oya in January-July, or a modest pace of 1.9%oya in July. A further breakdown shows moderation in manufacturing FAI (8.3%oya in July, vs. 9.3% in June) and further slowing in infra FAI (2.0%oya in July, vs. 4.6% in June), along with ongoing sluggishness in real estate investment (falling 10.8%oya, vs. -10.1% in June) (Figure 3). By ownership, private investment stayed weak, with 0.0%oya growth in January-July, while public investment rose 6.3%oya.

Figure 3: FAI growth by industry
![](images/1fcbb3820ca4eecb809300edb74d84ac0ea5d656d093fc0ff9f00a814d25efaa.jpg)

The soft July activity data reinforce the downside risk to our current quarter growth forecast we recently warned about. In our previous forecast, we penciled in a policy shift after the April Politburo meeting that would support a recovery to trend growth in 2H (4.5%q/q saar on average). However, the policy effect has so far been absent, either because the policy easing is not sufficient or due to a lag in policy action or transmission.

The housing policy easing in May temporarily improved new home sales and new home starts in June, followed by renewed weakness in July. Infra FAI growth slowed to 2.0%oya in July, the weakest monthly reading since 2022. This is related to the slow pace of special local government bond issuance this year, attributable to limited pool of qualified projects and the lifetime personal accountability scheme for government officials in investment projects. The July Politburo meeting emphasized that counter-cyclical policy measures should be strengthened, and boosting consumption received higher policy priority, especially support for service consumption. Then last week the State Council announced 20 measures to support consumption. The NDRC mentioned that some proceeds from special Treasury bonds and special LGB could be part of this support, but has provided no details.

## Housing time inconsistency issues persist

Although the housing market is undergoing its most severe downturn in three decades, the policy adjustment has remained gradual, creating two major time inconsistencies. First, slowing the price adjustment to protect household net worth has prolonged the quantity adjustment. Second, the housing drag on growth remains significant, and cannot be offset by replacement drivers such as high-end manufacturing.

The NBS 70 cities’ new home prices were down 5.3%oya or 0.6%m/m nsa, and secondary home prices were down 8.2%oya or 0.8%m/m nsa in July. Compared to the 2021 peak, new home starts (in floor space) contracted 65% in July, while new home sales dropped 52%. Meanwhile, new home prices declined 7.7%, and secondary home prices dropped 13.9% (Figure 4). As a sufficient price adjustment is still absent, fears of further future price declines remain entrenched. As in other asset markets, unless the market believes that prices have bottomed out and even overshot the adjustment, demand rarely recovers meaningfully.

Our view has been that the adjustment needs to be completed early through a comprehensive strategy, such as a large-scale real estate stability fund to address funding stresses for developers, a central government-led funding package to support private housing de-stocking and public housing development, and full removal of home purchase restrictions. The recently concluded 3rd Plenum made some headway on Hukou and rural land reform, but a concrete reform plan is awaited. We do not expect a major shift from the calibrated approach in the near term. The renewed weakness in housing activity may trigger additional housing policy relaxation, e.g., further loosening in home purchase restrictions in tier-1 cities and fiscal support (reduction of stamp duty or value-added tax). As such, we think China’s housing sector will remain a drag on growth in 2024, at another 1.2-1.4%pts, followed by a gradual stabilization in 2025.

![](images/551a002ab3f90351ae18435225193b91a2463991010879ffc6c7c8be063b4e6c.jpg)

## Revising 2H growth profile

Despite the absent policy effect, we think a policy shift has taken place, though the adjustment is gradual and calibrated; hence, the policy effect tends to be modest and likely delayed compared to our previous forecasts. In particular, acceleration of special LGB has lagged behind our expectations, but we maintain our view that this year’s quota will be fully issued, though it appears difficult to complete by the end of 3Q as in normal years (hence the policy effect will tend to be delayed into 4Q24 and 1Q25). Expanding the use of special LGB would be helpful to achieve this, and also helpful to improve fiscal policy effectiveness.

Overall, the delayed policy effect leads us to revisit the quarterly growth forecast. We are revising down the current quarter’s growth to 3%q/q saar (previously: 4%), and lift the forecast for 4Q growth to 5.5%q/q saar (previously: 5%), with 1Q25 growth at 4.3%q/q saar (previously: 3.7%). Our fullyear 2024 growth forecast is now 4.6% (previously: 4.7%), with 2025 growth at 4.0% (previously: 3.9%).

## Hong Kong: U rate unchanged at 3.0%

Hong Kong SAR’s unemployment rate stayed unchanged at 3.0% sa for the three months ending July. Total employment rose 5.3k to 3,708.6k, while the labor force rose 8.4k to 3,826.4k. Seasonally adjusted, total employment fell 0.2%m/ m sa by our estimate, while the labor force stayed unchanged in m/m sa terms. By sector breakdown, employment was up for public administration, social and personal services, finance, insurance, real estate and professional services sectors. However, it is a bit worrisome that employment in the retail, accommodation and food services sectors fell 4.7k (or -1.3%m/m sa), registering the 10<sup>th</sup> consecutive month of decline.

Business indicators remained subdued in July. The SME business receipt current diffusion index (DI) rose 0.4pt to a still weak level of 41.8, while the outlook DI fell 1.1pt to 45.4. SME employment DI fell 1.1pt to 48.3, suggesting further weakening of employment in SME businesses. Meanwhile, the total economy PMI rose 1.3pt to 49.5, while the employment component of PMI rose 0.3pt to 49.3, both staying below the 50-threshold. Overall, the city’s unemployment rate has returned to the lowest level since the onset of the pandemic, and we expect it to remain around 3% in the near term.

According to HK C&SD, the provisional estimate of HK population was 7,531.8k at mid-2024, compared with 7,527.9k at end-2023 and 7,536.1k at mid-2023. Note that the population has stayed above the pre-pandemic peak of 7,520.5k at end-2019 (Figure 5). During the period from mid-2023 to mid-2024, a natural decrease of 18.1k was recorded, together with a net inflow of 13.8k HK residents.

Figure 5: Hong Kong population
![](images/df81806858e644c71569b84b86898dca0762dfea5af378d7bcc1ad166095aeb9.jpg)

## Taiwan: 2Q grew 5.1% with a capex bounce

Taiwan’s 2Q GDP report shows the economy grew 5.06%oya (compared to 5.09%oya in the advanced 2Q GDP report), following upwardly revised 1Q GDP growth at 6.63%oya (previously: 6.56%oya). Seasonally-adjusted, GDP grew 1.16% q/q saar in 2Q, following 1Q growth of 0.4%. In a further breakdown, exports of goods and services showed moderate growth in 2Q, following solid sequential expansion in the previous four quarters. Net exports have been a drag on headline GDP through 1H24 on strong imports, which in turn partly reflects strong domestic fixed investment (hence boosting capital goods imports). Following notable contraction during the tech downcycle (3Q22-3Q23), the corporate sector

appears to have resumed capex significantly. Gross capital formation jumped 36.3% q/q saar in 2Q (vs +17.1% in 1Q) (Figure 6). Consumer spending grew moderately in 2Q, rising 2.0% q/q saar, following a similar expansion at 2.3% in 1Q.

Figure 6: Taiwan capital goods imports and gross capital formation %q/q saar, both scales
![](images/bd7bd8793e94d6d840c74517c73d0b0e3b2395323775df4ca9286558ed5d22ad.jpg)

Our baseline forecasts continue to look for a steady uptrend in Taiwan’s tech exports in the coming quarters, with ongoing structural strength in AI-related products and HPC-related demand, along with a gradual recovery in other tech demand areas, including PC, smartphone, and other consumer-related tech demand. The upturn in Taiwan’s domestic capex trend likely reflects Taiwan manufacturers’ constructive outlook on external demand conditions going into the coming quarters. Meanwhile, growth concerns on the global economy seem to be on the rise. Our global team has modestly raised the assessed risk of a 2H25 US/global recession to 35%. On the domestic front, the steady upturn in employment growth (and hence household income), as well as a notable uptick in consumer confidence and solid pickup in the non-manufacturing PMI, points to solid domestic demand growth going into 2H24.

Taking all factors together, as long as the US/global economies avoid a recession (which is our current global view), we expect the Taiwan economy to grow steadily ahead, averaging 2.6% q/q saar in 2H, reflecting a steady upturn in export sector activity and industrial activity feeding through to a decent expansion in domestic demand. Overall, our forecast for 2024 full-year GDP growth is unchanged at 4.2%y/y.

## Korea

• Unemployment rate fell back to cyclical low again

## • Customs exports rose in early August

Employment data do not suggest an easing in labor market conditions, with a cyclically low unemployment rate. Both employment and the labor force marked a contraction in three-month trend growth, yet the fall in the labor force led the unexpected drop in the jobless rate. Customs exports data show a rebound in exports flow in early August after July’s fall. Next week, we expect a rise in household leverage growth and a robust consumer sentiment index. In all, the Bank of Korea does not have a strong rationale for a policy pivot as of now, and we expect that the MPC will freeze the base rate. As a baseline case, we had expected that there would be dovish dissent vote(s) in August, but as a close call vs. unanimous freeze decision.

## Jobless rate fell in July

The seasonally adjusted unemployment rate unexpectedly fell 0.3%-pt to 2.5% (JPMe and consensus 2.9%) in July, back to the cyclical low (Figure 1). Employment growth was not particularly strong with a modest monthly employment gain (+0.1%m/m, sa) but a contraction in trend (-1.4%3m/3m, saar), yet the fall in labor participation led the drop in the jobless rate. The labor force fell 0.2%m/m, sa for the third monthly fall, and the labor force participation ratio fell to 64.2% from April’s 64.7% cyclical peak.

Figure 1: Korea labor market
![](images/325433383806e0a866a40e9cfb9e741ce4a37da88498432c731647a623d3dcc5.jpg)
Source: NSO, and J.P. Morgan

In the details, service sectors drove employment growth, as our tracking of private service employment rose 0.3%m/m, sa for the fourth monthly gain while manufacturing sector employment fell 0.5%. Among the service sectors, employment growth was robust in transportation (+1.2%m/m, sa in July and the seventh consecutive monthly gain) and financial (0.6%m/m, sa in July and the fourth monthly gain). Construction employment continued to fall, suggesting a continuing drag on the sector’s activity.

Figure 2: Wage and core CPI inflation
![](images/c8840735ed4e455fb02bccd7e3e97fe4c8d8e924953d378527c40a3fe00c8a8d.jpg)

In all, labor market conditions are not yet easing notably with the cyclically low jobless rate and a continued rise in service employment, while labor supply growth is likely to plateau. Such labor market conditions do not align well with the stabilizing core (service) inflation trend, likely as this trend may reflect more of easing of supply side price pressure (such as energy prices) by pass-through, not the trend in wage growth. To be sure, the available data for hourly wage growth suggest that nominal wage growth has been holding up at around a 4% annualized pace in recent months (Figure 2).

## Customs exports rose in early August

Customs exports during the first 10 days of August rose 16.7%oya, despite fewer trading days this month than a year ago. As a result, we estimate that full-month exports should rise 21.4%oya in August vs. 13.9%oya actual growth in July. If seasonally adjusted, the first 10-day exports rebounded 5.5%m/m, sa after a 5.6% fall in July (although it is a noisy 10-day series, not the actual full month series), and such a rebound in August exports would imply 13.2%3m/3m, saar trend growth in August (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Korea customs exports
![](images/5f9fd95026a155010bf5906d431a564534e15839effa47b1493113ab815a14e2.jpg)

The external demand outlook has been unclear amid uncertainty about growth trends in major trading partners, yet signals suggest Korea’s near-term exports growth has been holding up, including July’s manufacturing PMI and early August customs trade data. Meanwhile, J.P. Morgan estimates customs imports will rise 17.9%oya and 1.0%m/m, sa in August, indicating that the customs trade balance should hold up despite the seasonal drag in the month.

## India

• Favourable base effects drive down CPI inflation even as food inflation outlook improves

• WPI inflation eases in July; food prices sequentially increase but core prices soften

• June IP disappoints led by manufacturing

Headline inflation in July expectedly fell driven by large favourable base effects despite a sequential increase in food prices. Looking ahead, a meaningful pickup in the monsoon is driving a broad-based fall in food prices which is likely to contain the headline inflation for 3Q24 to below 4%—below the RBI’s estimate.

On the other hand, June IP disappointed, printing much below expectations led by softer manufacturing, consumer durables, and non-durables—increasing the likelihood of sub-7%oya GDP growth for 2Q24.

## CPI inflation gaps down on base effects but food price outlook improves

CPI inflation expectedly gapped down to below 4% in July— printing at 3.5%oya (J.P. Morgan and Consensus: 3.6%) from 5.1% in June—but helped by large favourable base effects (Figure 1). On a sequential basis, headline CPI prices rose 0.9%m/m, sa underpinned by a large 1.5% (m/m, sa) sequential increase in food prices. But the July monthly increase in food prices is deceptive, because it was largely led by vegetable prices which had stabilized and begun to decline towards the end of July itself and this dynamic has continued into August. In fact, even outside vegetables, other food prices (cereals, pulses) have shown a relatively benign outturn in August thus far. With the monsoon tracking 5% above normal—notwithstanding large special variations—healthy sowing and softer global food prices, the outlook for food inflation is looking progressively more constructive. At last week’s RBI policy review, we had indicated that we saw downside risks to the RBI’s 4.4% inflation forecast for this quarter. The July CPI outturn and August tracking simply increase our conviction of that and, barring another food price shock, we see CPI averaging less than 4% in the current quarter. In turn, this creates downside risks for the RBI’s full-year inflation forecast of 4.5%.

Figure 1: Headline CPI inflation
![](images/224bea9b4426ae363bf04395d60bc7229285964779afd1fb088c2a9203e54cab.jpg)

Meanwhile, the soft run of core inflation continued in July. Core-core prices did rise by 0.4%m/m, sa in July but this was due to the (presumably one-time) mobile tariff hike that was observed in July. Net of the tariff hike, core-core prices rose a benign 0.3% m/m, sa in July—which is what the monthly average has been over the last six months (Figure 2). Even accounting for the mobile tariff increase, core-core inflation—on a year-on-year basis—just edged up to 3.5%oya. To be sure, the mobile tariff increase reflected in the July data was lower than we had envisaged. So we build in a further increase in the August report. Even accounting for this, corecore inflation is expected to print at 3.5%oya in August too.

Figure 2: Core-Core ex mobile tariffs
![](images/fa503a4c97bd249609f57aa5554ad37bd43f1da2cba0c611a590ae53db6f7a0c.jpg)

## June IP disappoints

On its part, June IP disappointed coming in at 4.2%oya, much lower than expectations (J.P. Morgan and Consensus: 5.4%) (Figure 3). In sequential terms, IP contracted 1.2% in June after rising by 1.6% in April and 1.2% in May. The weakness in June was led by manufacturing (-1.8%m/m, sa) on the production side, and consumer durables and non-durables on the demand side.

The IP report suggests meaningful softness in manufacturing activity in 2Q24—likely impacted by elections and the heatwave—with manufacturing sequential growth averaging just 0.1% in 2Q24 (April-June) vis-à-vis 0.5% in 1Q24. In our view, this increases the risk of 2Q GDP—which prints on August 30—dipping below 7%.

Figure 3: Industrial Production
![](images/2a2707c00a085e71ff2154f9b3603ce985938b28d8502e27b276a1803998477f.jpg)

## WPI inflation softens in July

Wholesale Price Index (WPI) inflation printed 2%oya in July, easing from 3.4%oya in June. However, sequentially, it gained 0.8% on the back of just 0.1%m/m sa (Figure 4). This was largely driven by stronger wholesale food prices (+2.7%m/m sa) with significant increases in vegetables, as well as higher base metal prices. That said, core prices eased sequentially led by softer crude prices even though they firmed up in over year ago terms due to unfavourable base effects.

## ASEAN

• Malaysia’s solid 2Q24 GDP led by domestic demand and exports

Private consumption strength to be sustained by stable labor market and demand-side measures

• Growth momentum to continue in 2H24, resulting in full-year GDP growth of 5%

Malaysia’s 2Q data have been positive, with strong domestic activity fueled by both domestic consumption and exports. Within, domestic private consumption has remained supportive despite some pullback from government consumption. This is evident across several indicators: motor vehicle sales, credit card billings, and domestic production.

## Domestic resilience continues

Malaysia’s 2Q GDP came in stronger than expected, rising 5.8%oya and 11%q/q, saar. The strong print reflects recent exports gains, a continued pick-up in capex, and strong private consumption (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Malaysia real GDP
![](images/a25dc3f1af39d183359a6152f8723df0d02cd9a7ffb68f64d689c704b682ce94.jpg)
After the 2022 recovery in PCE, non-discretionary consumption (e.g. F&B) has been growing faster than discretionary (e.g. recreation & culture, restaurants & hotels , Figure 2). However, since the turn of 2024 we are seeing spillover in discretionary spending as well.

![](images/5d72fd42a0886e439b1f3e34956e449ea343a645e4cdb724817d90f58b593419.jpg)

The spillover to discretionary consumption is evident from high frequency data which suggest motor vehicle sales are rising compared to pre-pandemic levels as well as in comparison with other ASEAN economies (Figure 3).

Figure 3: ASEAN motor vehicle sales
![](images/4c78f0aef74c6d26e285a6590f28f25f50e537e0dd91c187b709889c91e3057f.jpg)

## Drivers of domestic demand

In 2Q24, trade recovered strongly on back of global tech demand, and led to robust domestic production (Figure 4), which in turn improved income and employment in Malaysia. Thus, household consumption was underpinned by improving labour market conditions. Strong employment coupled with modest inflation not only created domestic demand but also led to an increase in household saving along with low overdue balances (Figure 5 and 6).

Figure 4: Malaysia industrial production by demand
![](images/fa081c7ba8a2f6bb07216fb112644df68418c47d924ab8be6c5a9e22c62d57f4.jpg)

Figure 5: Malaysia employment and household savings %2q, saar
![](images/1f66607c4cb5cae449919ba22f4f923d29d0e174bbe1ccbeeed05fb1735ffe57.jpg)

Figure 6: Malaysia credit card outstanding and overdue balances
![](images/c6dbf301eea8a0b27f257df63155fd98437e420b038f7398832c9d43b7292783.jpg)

## Demand-side measures to sustain private consumption

The government’s demand-side policy measures have also contributed to the expansion of private consumption. The flexibility to withdraw from the Employee Provident Fund (EPF) has boosted households’ liquidity and spending appetite. In May, the Fund announced a restructuring to allow members to hold an additional account from which withdrawals can be made flexibly; it also gave members a one-time option to transfer funds as an initial amount into this account. Additionally, members can now contribute 10% of their total EPF contribution every month to the new flexible account.

As of June 10, RM10.9 billion (or 0.6% of GDP) has been transferred as initial amounts into the flexible account and RM7 billion has been withdrawn from the flexible account (see link). The window to switch accounts ends in August. As such, we expect spill-overs into domestic consumption to persist through 2H24.

The government has also announced civil service salary hikes of 15%-43% as part of the upcoming Budget 2025 (see link), providing another impetus to private consumption through 2025.

## Fuel subsidy rationalization

The impact of the diesel subsidy rationalization (introduced in early June, Figure 7) on transport prices (0.5%m/m, sa; 1.2%oya) was subdued, given that it mostly affects the logistics and industrial sector.

Figure 7: Malaysia and Singapore retail fuel prices
![](images/47b42e335fcb9234d8cfba27ee8017584ff3a10e43f77aee881257e7a8b9c63a.jpg)

In our view, the bigger inflation catalyst on the horizon is the RON95 fuel subsidy rationalization, although the timing of the implementation remains uncertain. The normalization of retail fuel prices will likely exert sharp upward pressure on headline CPI, but the impact on core inflation could be manageable, given the limited corporate pricing power in Malaysia and the second-order effects of weaker real incomes on discretionary spending.

## Momentum to continue in 2H24

We expect private consumption growth to continue at its trend pace of 6%-7%q/q, saar, leading to full-year GDP growth at 5%oya. The RON95 subsidy rationalization, if it materializes, will likely lead to a one-off hit on real incomes and discretionary spending, but any slowdown could be buffered by recent demand-side measures (e.g., EPF withdrawal scheme, civil service salary increases).

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
